# The Sixteen Characters

---

### A note on this book

This is the third text of the canon. The first book, The Eight Characters, taught the grammar of a single chart by reading one from its first Stem to its last decade. The companion reference laid the same grammar out as tables, entry by entry. Neither had room for the question that most people bring to a chart before any other, which is not "who am I" but "who am I with." A person is written in eight characters. A marriage is written in sixteen, and this book is about the second eight and about what happens between the two.

It is built in two parts, each in the manner of the text it stands beside. Part One is a book: twelve chapters that argue, quote, and read, every one of them ending on the same pair of charts. Part Two is a reference: tables in the form of the companion reference, written so that any two charts can be looked up in them — any Day Master beside any other, any spouse palace beside any other, any year against a marriage. Part One writes pinyin with its tones, as the first book does, and quotes the classics in Chinese; Part Two writes plain letters and keeps its Chinese to names, as the reference does. Where this book says "the first book" it means The Eight Characters, and where it says "the reference" it means the companion reference, quoted here exactly as there.

Everything the first two texts established is assumed: the hidden Stems and their ranks, the five seasonal states, the relations among the Branches and their weighting by distance, the reverse life cycle of the yin Stems, the names of the Ten Gods in both of their sets. On one point of mechanism the classical sources quoted here read differently from those texts: whether a combination, like a clash, opens a storage Branch. The classics say it shuts one, this canon follows the classics, and the two earlier texts are to be corrected to agree; the difference is set out where it arises, in Chapter Nine. On other points — the old chapter on the Day Blade, the old books' word for a year that combines the Day Master — this book reports where the classical texts are harder on a chart than the first two texts were, and says so where it happens. A reader who has not met those should begin there. A reader who has will find that marriage needs no new machinery at all. It needs the old machinery pointed at one Branch, one or two Stems, and one other person.

### A note on the charts used in this book

The first book read one chart, and this one keeps it. The man was born in Helsinki on 29 June 1976 at 07:02 by the clock and about 06:38 by the sun:

| | Year | Month | Day | Hour |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Stem | 丙 Yang Fire | 甲 Yang Wood | 壬 Yang Water | 癸 Yin Water |
| Branch | 辰 Dragon | 午 Horse | 子 Rat | 卯 Rabbit |

His Luck Pillars run forward from the Month Pillar: 乙未, 丙申, 丁酉, 戊戌, 己亥, 庚子, 辛丑, 壬寅, 癸卯, 甲辰, turning in the early months of 1979 and of every tenth year after. The first book's diagnosis stands: a weak Yang Water Day Master, Imprisoned in the Horse month, seated on its Blade in a clash with that month, with no Metal anywhere; Useful God Metal, Auspicious God Water.

A book about two charts needs a second, and the second is also a real birth. The woman was born in Nanjing on 24 March 1980 at 04:02 by the clock. China kept Beijing time (UTC+8) without daylight saving in 1980, and Nanjing lies at about 118.8° east, so true solar time there runs about five minutes behind the clock; the equation of time on that date removes about six more. The birth falls at about 03:51 solar — inside the Tiger hour (03:00–05:00 solar), with more than an hour to spare before the Rabbit hour. Here, unlike in the man's case, the clock and the sun agree on the Hour Pillar.

| | Year | Month | Day | Hour |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Stem | 庚 Yang Metal | 己 Yin Earth | 丙 Yang Fire | 庚 Yang Metal |
| Branch | 申 Monkey | 卯 Rabbit | 申 Monkey | 寅 Tiger |

Because the subject is female and the Year Stem is yang, her Luck Pillars run backward from the Month Pillar, beginning at a little over six years of age: 戊寅, 丁丑, 丙子, 乙亥, 甲戌, 癸酉, 壬申, 辛未, 庚午, 己巳, turning in May 1986 and in the May of every tenth year after.

Two things should be said before the reading begins. The chapters read these two charts as a couple would be read — their palaces as a household, their years as a shared calendar — because that is the question the book was asked. And the book reads the charts, not the lives. It does not know when these two met, or what has passed between them, and it says so where the question arises; nothing in what follows is a statement about what has happened, only about what the sixteen characters hold and when the calendar touches them. Where a reading of a real second chart would have gone further than the texts allow, the book has stopped.

One further chart appears once, in Chapter Nine, as a counter-example, and it, unlike the two charts of the book, is a date and not a person: a woman born in Tampere on 15 May 1978 at 12:30 by the clock and about 12:09 by the sun, whose pillars are 戊午, 丁巳, 丁丑, 丙午.

All three charts were cast with the same engine and the same conventions as the first book's: true solar time for the hour and the day, the seasonal nodes for the month and the year.

### A note on sources

This book quotes, which the first book did not need to do. On marriage the tradition speaks in many voices across seven centuries, some of them wise and some of them cruel, and a reader deserves to hear which voice is speaking. The Chinese is given in simplified characters, as throughout the canon, with a translation beneath it; the translations are this book's own and aim at the sense rather than the syllable count.

The foundations are the same as before. 渊海子平 (Yuān Hǎi Zǐ Píng), the Song core in its Ming dress, gives the grammar of marriage inside one chart: the Day Stem as the self and the Day Branch as the spouse's place, Wealth as the wife of a man and Officer as the husband of a woman, and the first rules for when a marriage arrives. 三命通会 (Sān Mìng Tōng Huì), Wan Minying's compendium of 1578, gathers everything that had been said by then — the theory of the combinations as marriages, the chapters on clash, punishment and harm, the long treatise on the charts of women — and gathers it uncritically, which is its value and its danger. 子平真诠 (Zǐ Píng Zhēn Quán), under Shen Xiaozhan's name, supplies the rule that the spouse's palace and the spouse's star must be read together and both of them under the chart's structure. 滴天髓 (Dī Tiān Suǐ), with its old notes and with Ren Tieqiao's 阐微, supplies the deepest correction the tradition ever made to itself on this subject: that the spouse is a function in the chart before it is a star.

Four further sources matter here that the first book had no reason to name. 神峰通考 (Shén Fēng Tōng Kǎo), also printed as 命理正宗, by Zhang Nan 张楠 of the Ming, contains the one chapter in the classical canon that is explicitly about matching two charts, 男女合婚说, and a blistering attack on the matching that everyone else practiced. 命理约言 (Mìng Lǐ Yuē Yán), by Chen Su'an 陈素庵 in the first decades of the Qing, repeats the attack, counts its human cost, and states the positive rule in a single sentence. 协纪辨方书 (Xié Jì Biàn Fāng Shū), the almanac compendium compiled by imperial order under the Qianlong emperor, examined the popular methods of marriage matching one by one, found each of them a fabrication, and struck them from the calendar. And 命理探原 (Mìng Lǐ Tàn Yuán), by Yuan Shushan 袁树珊 in the first years of the Republic, drew the consequence the earlier critics had left implicit and wrote down the method of comparing two charts element against element. Older than all of these, and quoted where they bear on the question, are the Song collection 五行精纪 (Wǔ Xíng Jīng Jì) and the commentaries on the 珞琭子 rhapsody, which preserve what marriage reading looked like before the Day Master became the center of the chart; and older still, for what marriage is, the 易 (Yì) and the 礼记 (Lǐ Jì).

Two honesties about these sources govern the whole book.

The first is that the classical canon does not contain a finished system for comparing two charts. It contains a thorough method for reading marriage inside one chart, a handful of explicit statements about choosing between charts, and a logic — disease and medicine, excess and deficiency — that extends to two charts as naturally as it applies to one. This book states the classical rules as rules, quotes them, and says who said them. Where it extends the logic further than any classical author did, it says so in the sentence where it happens. The reader will always know which ground is underfoot.

The second concerns the text. For most passages the quotations here follow the received transcriptions, compared against one another. For Zhang Nan's chapters on matching, the circulating transcriptions are corrupt in places that change the meaning — one of them has him blaming failed marriages on the clear sight of the matchmakers, where the printed text blames their lack of it — and this book follows a printed Shanghai edition held by the National Library of China. Where editions disagree and the disagreement touches the doctrine, the disagreement is noted. Plain slips of the transcribers — one character written for another of like shape or sound — are corrected without comment where the sense and the parallel clause leave no doubt.

### A note on man and woman

The sources speak of husband and wife, 夫妇 (fū fù), and of no other couple. They assign the spouse a different star according to the sex of the person whose chart it is: for a man, the element he controls; for a woman, the element that controls her. This book keeps that assignment, because it is the tradition's, and because Chapter One will argue that it is less crude than it sounds. Everything else in these pages — the palace, the meeting of two Day Masters, the exchange of elements between two charts, the years — is the same for anyone. For couples the sources did not imagine, there is no classical rule to say which star stands for the spouse, and this book does not invent one. The palace, the reading by function, the exchange and the years need no such rule, and they are the larger part of the method.

The sources also speak from inside a world that is not ours. It had concubines, and so the texts distinguish a principal wife from a secondary one. It forbade a widow to remarry with honor, and so the texts treat a second husband as a catastrophe. It measured a woman by her husband and her sons, and so the long chapters on women's charts spend their best ingenuity on those two questions and their worst on sorting women into the chaste and the unchaste. The technique underneath is sound and the social furniture on top of it is not, and the two can be separated, because the tradition's own finest authors had already begun to separate them — Chen Su'an by refusing the stars of ill omen, Ren Tieqiao by warning that a woman's chart must never be lightly read for vice, the old notes to 滴天髓 by dismissing in one line most of what the manuals said about women. This book follows them. Where a classical phrase is hard — 克妻 (kè qī), to overcome the wife; 克夫 (kè fū), to overcome the husband — it is translated as it stands and then read for what it measures, which is always a strain on a relation and never a sentence on a person.

---

# Part One — The Book

---

## Chapter One — Husband and Wife

Eight characters describe one person. That was the whole claim of the first book, and it was enough to fill it: one instant, four pillars, a self at the center and a world arranged around it. But nobody lives as a diagram of one. The first question most people put to a chart is not about the self at all. It is about the other chair at the table — whether it will be filled, by whom, when, and what the two of them will make of each other across a life. A marriage is written in sixteen characters, eight and eight, and the wish to read them together is older than the art of reading the one.

It is older because, in the worldview that produced this system, the couple is older than the person. The modern mind begins with individuals and derives relationships from them, as arrangements two finished people choose to enter. The tradition begins the other way round. Its account of how there came to be anything human at all is a sentence from the 序卦 (Xù Guà), the Sequence of the Hexagrams, in the 易:

> 有天地然后有万物，有万物然后有男女，有男女然后有夫妇，有夫妇然后有父子。
>
> There is heaven and earth, and only then the ten thousand things; the ten thousand things, and only then man and woman; man and woman, and only then husband and wife; husband and wife, and only then father and child.

Read the order. Parent and child come after the couple, not before. Ruler and subject, elder and younger, every vertical line of authority the old society would later draw, hangs from this one horizontal relation. The same text gives the reason the hexagram of duration follows the hexagram of attraction: 夫妇之道，不可以不久也 — the way of husband and wife may not be other than lasting. 咸 (xián), attraction, is glossed as 感, to be moved: two breaths responding to each other and so giving themselves to each other. 恒 (héng) is glossed as 久, to last. Marriage, in the oldest stratum of the tradition, is those two words in that order. Something moves; then something holds.

The ritual books say the same in the language of households. The 礼记, in the chapter 昏义 (Hūn Yì), defines the wedding rite as 将合二姓之好 — the joining of two family names in friendship, to serve the ancestral temple above and continue the generations below; and in another chapter it calls the rite 万世之始, the beginning of ten thousand generations, placing it directly after the sentence "heaven and earth join, and afterward the ten thousand things arise." The 中庸 says that the way of the exemplary person 造端乎夫妇 — makes its first beginning among husbands and wives — and at its furthest reach is displayed in heaven and earth. And the Han court's digest of doctrine, the 白虎通 (Bái Hǔ Tōng), explains the word for wife by its sound: 妻者，齐也，与夫齐体 — "wife" (qī) means "level" (qí): she is of one body with her husband, and level with him. The society that copied that sentence did not always live by it. The sentence is there all the same.

What all of these texts are doing is taking the first idea of the first book's second chapter and giving it a human face. Yin and Yang, that chapter said, are not two substances but the two hands of a single force, and nothing comes to exist except through their alternation. 一阴一阳之谓道: one yin, one yang — that is called the Way. Man and woman are that sentence walking about. 男女构精，万物化生: the essences of male and female are joined, and the ten thousand things take form and are born. A marriage, in this account, is not a contract between two completed beings. It is the place where the basic act of the cosmos is carried on at human scale, and for that reason it is the place where a human life is most exposed to the quality of its own time. The first book called the chart a letter from the cosmos with a name on the envelope. Marriage is the part of the letter that is addressed to two.

### How the Stems marry

The system of the Eight Characters did not leave this as philosophy. It built marriage into its own alphabet, and the place to see it is in the Five Stem Combinations — the pairs 甲己, 乙庚, 丙辛, 丁壬, 戊癸 that the first book introduced as attractions between a yang Stem and the yin Stem five places on. 三命通会, in its chapter on the combinations, says what kind of attraction it is:

> 夫合者，乃和谐之义。如阳见阳，二阳相竞则为克；阴见阴，二阴不足则为克；唯阴见阳，阳见阴为合，亦如男女相合而成夫妇之道。
>
> Combination means harmony. When yang meets yang, the two contend, and that is conflict; when yin meets yin, the two fall short, and that is conflict; only when yin meets yang and yang meets yin is there combination — just as man and woman join, and the way of husband and wife is made.

And then it tells the five combinations as five weddings. Wood fears Metal, which fells it. So Yang Wood, the elder brother, takes his younger sister Yin Wood and marries her into the Metal clan as the wife of Yang Metal: 甲兄遂将乙妹嫁金家，与庚为妻 — and that is why 乙 and 庚 combine. Fire fears Water; Yang Fire marries his sister Yin Fire to Yang Water, and 丁 and 壬 combine. Earth fears Wood; Yang Earth gives Yin Earth to Yang Wood, and 甲 and 己 combine. Metal fears Fire; Yang Metal gives Yin Metal to Yang Fire, and 丙 and 辛 combine. Water fears Earth; Yang Water gives Yin Water to Yang Earth, and 戊 and 癸 combine.

It is a little fable of dynastic politics, and it would be easy to smile at it and move on. It is worth stopping instead, because the whole doctrine of the spouse in this system is folded inside it.

Look at who marries whom. In every one of the five pairs, the husband's element is the element that controls the wife's: Wood over Earth, Metal over Wood, Fire over Metal, Water over Fire, Earth over Water. The marriage is made across the controlling cycle, never across the generating one. And in every pair the two Stems are of opposite polarity, a yang husband and a yin wife. Now recall how the first book derived the Ten Gods. The element a Day Master controls, in the opposite polarity, is Direct Wealth (正财 zhèng cái). The element that controls a Day Master, in the opposite polarity, is Direct Officer (正官 zhèng guān). Stand where the husband stands in any of the five weddings, and the Stem beside him is his Direct Wealth. Stand where the wife stands, and the Stem beside her is her Direct Officer. The tradition's two definitions — the wife of a man is his Wealth; the husband of a woman is her Officer — are not two rules. They are one relation, seen from its two ends, and the five combinations are its five instances. The fable was not an illustration added to the theory. The theory is the fable, generalized.

The second thing folded into the story is its motive. The elder brother gives his sister to the clan he fears. Marriage here is a peace: the conversion of a threat into kinship. Between elements, control is the relation that could become injury — the axe and the tree, the flood and the flame — and combination is what that same relation becomes when the two parties are of opposite charge and choose alliance. An old commentator on the 珞琭子 rhapsody, the monk Tanying, compressed it into fourteen characters: 五行相生为父子，阴阳相制为夫妻. Where the phases generate one another, there is parent and child; where yin and yang hold one another in check, there is husband and wife. Generation is the vertical relation, the one that hands substance down. Mutual restraint is the horizontal one. A parent gives you what you are made of. A spouse gives you your shape.

This is the least sentimental definition of marriage in any literature, and the modern reader's first instinct is to resent it — to hear, in "the wife is what the man controls," only the household law of a dead empire. The law was real. 子平真诠, explaining why Direct Wealth stands for the wife, reaches for the slogan of that law without embarrassment: 夫为妻纲, the husband is the wife's mainstay. This book will not pretend the slogan away. But it asks the reader to hear the mechanics underneath it with the ear the first book trained. Control, that book said, is regulation, not destruction — the thermostat that keeps the furnace from running away. What I control is what I am answerable for, what I tend and give form to, what I must be strong enough to hold without crushing. What controls me is what gives me form, holds me to a line, and makes of my energy something the world can use. Those are descriptions of what any two people in a lasting bond do for each other in turn, and the chart of every person, man or woman, contains both relations. The tradition fixed one of them on the husband and the other on the wife. The relations themselves do not care who holds which end.

And the system has its own correction built in, which the slogan-makers never noticed. The same chart that calls a man's wife his Wealth — a thing governed — places her in the Branch beneath his own Day Stem. That Branch is the spouse palace, and the first book said what it is: the seat of the self, the ground of daily life, the foundation the Day Master stands on. A man does not stand over his spouse palace. He stands on it. The star says what the self reaches toward and must carry. The palace says what carries the self. Every chart holds both statements at once, and a reading that hears only the first has read half of the marriage.

### The marriage inside one chart

That last point is the one this book will lean on hardest, so it should be made plainly. The Day Pillar is already a couple. The entry-level manual in 渊海子平 says it in the fewest words anyone has managed:

> 日干为己身，日支为妻妾。
>
> The Day Stem is one's own self; the Day Branch is wife and concubine.

A Stem above, a Branch below; the visible, initiating self and the ground that receives it. The first book catalogued the five ways a Day Master can relate to its own seat — rooted in it, fed by it, pressed by it, giving to it, governing it — and remarked that these color a person's relationship to themselves and to their partner for a lifetime. That was not a figure of speech. Before anyone has met anyone, every person is already living inside a marriage of one Stem and one Branch, and has been since birth. The habits of that first marriage — whether the self expects to be held or expects to hold, expects a mirror or a stranger underfoot — are what a second person walks into.

So when the tradition reads "marriage" in a chart, it is reading three different things, and most confusion about the subject comes from running them together. There is a place: the spouse palace, the Day Branch, the room in the chart where the closest partnership lives. There is a relation: the spouse star, the Wealth of a man or the Officer of a woman, wherever in the chart it happens to stand. And there is a time: the Luck Pillars and the years, which bring the star, or stir the palace, or do neither, in an order that differs for every birth. The palace says what the marriage rests on. The star says what kind of bond it is and how well the self can bear it. The years say when. The chapters that follow take the place and the relation one at a time and then together, first for anyone and then for a man and for a woman; the years are kept for the end, when there are two charts to set against them.

Only after all of that does a second chart enter. This is the order of the classical authors, and it is the opposite of the order in which marriage matching is usually sold. The marketplace begins with two people and asks whether they "match." The tradition begins with one person and asks what marriage is in that chart — what it rests on, what it costs, what it needs, when it comes — and then asks the same of the other, and only then sets the two beside each other to see what each does to the other's balance. A chart that cannot carry a marriage is not rescued by a clever pairing, and a chart that can is not ruined by an unlucky one. The second chart matters. It matters second.

One caution belongs here, at the threshold, and it is the first book's caution doubled. The chart illuminates; it does not command — and a reading of two charts illuminates two freedoms, not one. Nothing in these pages can say whether two people should marry, or should stay. It can say where the current between them runs easily and where it meets rock, what each will tend to give and tend to take, and in which years the weather on the crossing will be rough. The classical authors, at their best, knew exactly this much and claimed no more. One of them, having laid out the whole method of choosing, ended his chapter with a sentence this book will come back to: what follows the wedding, good or ill, may be left to Heaven.

### The charts

Two Day Pillars, then, before anything else.

His is 壬子, Yang Water on the Rat. The first book spent a chapter on it: the ocean over the deepest trench, the self seated on its own element at its Emperor's Peak, the Blade beneath the Day Master, and inside the Rat a single hidden Stem, Yin Water — the Rival, living in the spouse palace. The marriage this man was born into, before he met anyone, is a marriage of water with water: a self that expects its closest ground to be made of what it is made of.

Hers is 丙申 (bǐng shēn), Yang Fire on the Monkey. The reference gives the pairing in a sentence: the sun melting raw ore. The Monkey is early autumn, and its principal Stem is Yang Metal, the element Fire controls; the Stem sits on what it governs. Inside the Monkey are three hidden Stems — Yang Metal as principal, Yang Water as secondary, Yang Earth as residual — and to a Yang Fire they are Windfall, the Merciless, and the Provider. The reference finishes the sentence: the ore fights back by releasing water as it melts. The marriage she was born into is a marriage of the sun with what it works on: a self that expects its closest ground to be something it shapes, and finds that the ground answers.

Already the two are different creatures. He sits on more of himself. She sits on what she governs, and on what governs her, in one Branch.

Now ask the old story about each of them. In the fable of the five weddings, Yang Water is a husband: Yang Fire gave him his sister, and the wife of 壬 is 丁, Yin Fire. That is his Direct Wealth, and the first book found where she lives in his chart — hidden in the Horse, the Month Branch, the very Branch that clashes his seat. Yang Fire is a husband in the same fable; he takes 辛, Yin Metal, as his wife. The story has no part for a woman born under a yang Stem. Her husband must be found by the rule the fable generalizes into, and the rule gives her 癸, Yin Water, as the Officer proper and 壬, Yang Water, as its fiercer form, the Seven Killings. Search her eight characters. There is no 癸 in them. There is 壬, twice, hidden: once in the Monkey of her Year, and once in the Monkey beneath her own Day Stem. The husband star of her chart lives in her spouse palace, in the form the old books called irregular, and Zhang Nan's ruling for exactly this case is plain: 若无正官，即以七杀为正夫 — where there is no Direct Officer, take the Seven Killings as the husband proper.

Look across at the man. His Day Master is 壬. He is, in person, the Stem that stands hidden in her room.

Now notice what these two Day Masters are to each other, because it is both what the old story would have chosen and what it says cannot be. Water controls Fire. In the monk's fourteen characters they stand in the relation of mutual restraint, which is the relation of husband and wife; by the derived rule she is his Wealth and he is her Authority, each the other's spouse star in person. But both are yang. The compendium's sentence on combination was that when yang meets yang the two contend, and 丙 and 壬 do not combine; they are one of the four pairs of Stems that clash. Each is the other's spouse star in the indirect form — she his Windfall, he her Merciless — and the bond between the two characters is not the fable's wedding but its quarrel. One more thing stands in his chart that bears on this. His Year Stem is 丙. The first book read it as the ocean facing the sun across the chart, Wealth at the widest horizon. The sun on his horizon has her Day Master's character.

The marketplace would have stopped there, or would not have looked this far. The tradition's best authors did neither. What they made of a pair like this one — a man whose wife star is his burden and stands in person before him, a woman whose husband star is in her own room in the form the old books distrusted, and between the two of them not the combination the story celebrates but the clash it warns of — is the argument of this book.

---

## Chapter Two — The Matchmakers

For most of the history of this art, the person who read a marriage in the stars was not a scholar and had not asked the couple. The ritual books lay out a wedding as six rites, and the 昏义 lists the first five in a breath — 纳采、问名、纳吉、纳征、请期: the proposal with its gift, the asking of the name, the report of a favorable omen, the betrothal presents, the request for a date — before the sixth, 亲迎 (qīn yíng), when the groom goes in person to fetch the bride. The second and third are where the diviner stood. 问名 (wèn míng), asking the name, meant carrying the girl's particulars home to be laid before the ancestors and tested; 纳吉 (nà jí), bringing the good omen, meant coming back to say the test had been passed. By late imperial times the particulars were written on a card, the 庚帖 (gēng tiě), which carried the year, month, day and hour of birth in Stems and Branches; the two families exchanged cards through a go-between; and the testing had a name of its own, 合婚 (hé hūn), joining for marriage — or, in the phrase everyone used, 合八字 (hé bā zì), fitting the eight characters together.

It is a phrase with an irony in it. What the matchmakers of the marketplace fitted together was almost never eight characters and eight. It was one and one, or two and two: the years, sometimes the years and the months. This chapter is about what they did, why they did it, and why the authors this book follows called it a fraud — because the method taught here was built, quite consciously, on the ruins of theirs.

### What the marketplace did

The popular art of matching had five instruments, and a reader who has grown up anywhere near the tradition will recognize all of them, since they are still in use.

The first was the system of the Three Cycles and Nine Palaces (三元九宫 sān yuán jiǔ gōng). The year of a man's birth was converted, by a table that shifts with each sixty-year cycle, into one of nine numbered palaces, and the year of a woman's birth into another; each number was a trigram; and the two trigrams were paired by a rule borrowed from the geomancers. Eight results were possible. Three of them — Life Breath, Heavenly Physician, Blessing and Virtue — made a superior match. Three — Severed Body, Wandering Soul, Returning Soul — made a middling one. Two — Five Ghosts and Severed Fate — made a match to be refused. The whole apparatus went under the name of Lü Cai 吕才, a scholar of the early Tang.

The second instrument needed no table at all. It was the zodiac. Every child knew the jingle about the six unlucky pairs: the horse offends the ox, the sheep and the rat are finished in a morning, the snake and the tiger are crossed knives, the dragon and the rabbit weep together, the cock fears the dog, the pig and the monkey do not last. Those are simply the Six Harms as the reference lists them — 午丑, 未子, 巳寅, 辰卯, 酉戌, 亥申 — applied to the Year Branches of two people and to nothing else; and beside them, for good measure, the six clashes, so that a Rat should not marry a Horse nor a Tiger a Monkey.

The third instrument was the oldest. It took the sound-element of the birth year — 纳音 (nà yīn), the scheme that gives every pair of years in the sixty-fold cycle an element of its own, Sea Metal and Furnace Fire and the rest — and treated that element as the person. A man's wife was whatever his year-element controlled; a woman's husband was whatever controlled hers. The spouse-element was then run through the twelve life stages against the month of the person's own birth. Born in a month where the spouse-element is between Birth and Decline, a man "increases wealth" for the household he marries into, and so does a woman; born where it is between Sickness and Nurture, each "drains" it. Born in the month of its Extinction, the man is a widower at the gate and the woman a widow at the gate — fated to lose a betrothed before the wedding. Born in the months of its Death and Tomb, the spouse "has many perils."

The fourth was a crowd of named evils, each fixed by the Branch of the birth year and one month of the calendar: the Broken Marrow, the Iron Broom, the Eight Defeats and the Great Defeat, the Scattering, the Lonely Star for men and the Widow's Lodge for women, the Clash of Womb against Womb. A girl born in a Pig, Goat or Dog year and in the third month carried the Eight Defeats, and that was that.

The fifth came after the match was approved: the Month of Great Profit, counted off from the bride's birth year, in which alone the wedding could be held without harming, in turn, the go-between, the groom's parents, the bride's parents, the groom, or the bride herself.

Look at what all five have in common. Not one of them uses the day. Not one of them uses the hour. The first two use the year alone; the others add the month. And the third — this is the detail that gives the game away — never compares the two people at all. It pronounces on the man from the man's year and month, and on the woman from hers, and the families simply take whichever verdict frightens them more.

### Where it came from

The core of it was not invented by fools. It is a fossil. The first book told how the reading of lives began with the Year Pillar as the center — Li Xuzhong's method in the Tang, in which the year was the self and the other pillars its circumstances — and how Xu Ziping, at the turn of the Song, moved the self to the Stem of the day. The older art did not vanish when the learned abandoned it. It went on living where it had always lived, in almanacs and in the mouths of go-betweens, for the simple reason that everyone knows the year they were born in and hardly anyone, in a village, could be sure of the hour.

The Song collection 五行精纪 still shows the older art in good order. "The position of the wife," it says, quoting a source already old, "is whatever one's own sound-element controls; if it stands in a region of vigor, the wife will be worthy and gentle and the two will keep each other to the end." That is the third instrument, stated as respectable doctrine at the end of the twelfth century. The control relation is the same one this book's first chapter found inside the five combinations. Only the self is in a different place: in the year, shared with every other child of that year and the next, instead of in the day.

So the gap between the scholars' art and the matchmakers' art is not a gap between learning and superstition. It is a gap of six hundred years. The marketplace was practicing, in the Ming and the Qing and after, a method the tradition itself had outgrown in the Song.

### Four refutations

The scholars said so, and they did not say it gently.

Zhang Nan, in the sixteenth century, put his attack at the front of 神峰通考, among the "erroneous doctrines" he meant the book to bury. On matching by the two years:

> 安可只以男女二年命，舍去月与日时，而能论人婚配者乎？
>
> How can anyone take only the two birth years of a man and a woman, throw away the month and the day and the hour, and still claim to judge a marriage?

On the named evils:

> 盖论人之祸福，当以年月日时四字俱全，更加天干地支所藏配合，论人休咎尚不可得，而以年月两字，不与日时相关，断头绝脚为说。
>
> To speak of a person's fortune one needs year, month, day and hour, all four, and with them the Stems and what the Branches hide and how these combine — and even then good and ill can hardly be got at. Yet these take two characters, year and month, cut loose from day and hour: a doctrine with its head lopped off and its feet severed.

He had evidence, and it is the most human paragraph in his book. He had many times, he writes, met old women, rich and honored, husbands and sons all living, who as girls had carried the Eight Defeats. Their parents had quietly falsified the year of birth in order to marry them off, and the truth was told to husband and sons only at the deathbed, so that the genealogy could be written correctly. When he cast the true charts he found exactly what the lives had shown: husband star and child star fine, the whole in balance. The evil star had done nothing. The only thing it had ever caused was the lie.

And on the idea, built into the third instrument, that a bride can drain or swell the fortune of the house she enters, he wrote the sentence that ought to stand over the door of every matchmaker:

> 且进财退财，系乎自己命运所招，安有他人家男女而能致我之祸福耶？
>
> Whether wealth comes or goes hangs on what one's own fate and luck have summoned. How could the son or daughter of another family be the cause of my fortune or my ruin?

A century later Chen Su'an, in 命理约言, went through the same ground more coolly and counted. In any one month, he pointed out, girls are born by the uncountable thousand; can every one of them be a widow? He had examined the charts of women who lived long with their husbands in wealth and honor, and a great many of them carried the Defeats and the Widow's Lodge. Then he named what the doctrine cost:

> 若不及辟之，或婚姻将谐而被破，或夫妇已配而相怨，或翁姑因此而憎弃。
>
> If this is not refuted in time, then matches on the point of being made are broken by it; or husband and wife, already joined, come to resent each other because of it; or a bride's parents-in-law come to loathe her and cast her off because of it.

The third refutation carried the authority of the throne. In 1739 the Qianlong emperor ordered his astronomers and ritual officials to sift the entire inherited lore of the almanac, and the resulting compendium, 协纪辨方书, devotes the first entry of its final book — the book of "errors exposed" — to marriage matching and the Month of Great Profit. Of all the ways the diviners have found to delude the age and cheat the people, it begins, none is worse than these two. Then:

> 夫妇之道，人伦之始。《书》载厘降，《诗》咏《关雎》，未尝有合婚之说也。
>
> The way of husband and wife is the beginning of all human relations. The Documents record the sending down of the two daughters; the Odes sing of the ospreys; nowhere is there any doctrine of marriage matching.

The compilers took the instruments apart one after another. The Nine Palaces rule was lifted from geomancy, where it prizes exactly the pairings that marriage should avoid. The Widow's Lodge was a misread term from the art of war. The verdicts of "draining" and "increasing" came from running the year's sound-element through the life stages — and here the imperial text makes the point that matters most for this book. The true art of fate, it says, does take the wife of a man as what his Stem controls and the husband of a woman as what controls hers, but it reckons from the Day Stem, not from the sound of the year; the diviners took the rule and stretched it onto the year. As for the result, the compilers did the arithmetic: twenty-five thousand nine hundred and twenty distinct charts fall within any three such months, and it is beyond all reason that every one of them should bereave a spouse. And the attribution was a forgery. The Tang History's own life of Lü Cai shows a man who spent his career demolishing exactly this kind of doctrine; the system had been fathered, after his death, on the court's most famous skeptic.

They saw the human cost as Chen had. People, they wrote, never ask why any of it should be so; they simply require that a match satisfy every test at once, and so it comes about that many pass the proper age without being able to marry at all. The entry closes on a line of bureaucratic finality: by memorial, the deletion has now been approved. They added one observation a historian can be grateful for: matching by the year was a custom of the common people of the north; the gentry, and the south, had never much believed in it.

The fourth voice is Yuan Shushan's, in the first years of the Republic, and he added little to the demolition, since little was left standing. What he added was the thing the first three had only gestured at: a method. That belongs to Chapter Eight.

### What they put in its place

A critic who only destroys leaves the field to whoever was there before. Two of the critics said what should be done instead, and their answers, set side by side, are the charter of this book.

Zhang Nan's comes in two parts. The first is a pair of tests, one for each family:

> 男之择女也，八字贵看夫子二星；女之择男也，八字贵得中和之道。
>
> When the man's side chooses a woman, what is prized in her eight characters is the pair of stars, husband and child; when the woman's side chooses a man, what is prized in his eight characters is that they have attained the way of central harmony.

Yuan Shushan, repeating the pair three centuries later, supplies the reasons the original left unsaid: where the husband and child stars are sound, 其福必优, her blessings will be ample; where a man's chart leans to neither side, 其寿必长, his life will be long. They are the reasons of a family that feared its daughter's widowhood before anything else.

The second is a rule for setting one chart against another, and it is the only such rule any classical author states outright:

> 但当看男命带比肩劫财重者，必择女命带伤官食神重者配之；若女命带伤官食神重者，必择男命带比肩劫财重者配之。此系合婚之正理。
>
> One need only look: where a man's chart is heavy with Companion and Rob Wealth, a woman whose chart is heavy with Hurting Officer and Eating God must be chosen to pair with him; and where a woman's chart is heavy with Hurting Officer and Eating God, a man whose chart is heavy with Companion and Rob Wealth must be chosen to pair with her. This is the true principle of marriage matching.

Chen Su'an's answer is a single sentence, and it is the calmest thing anyone in this tradition ever wrote about marriage:

> 总之男家择女，女家择夫，只照四柱常理，取其中和平顺者而已。婚后吉凶，听之于天可也。
>
> In sum: when the man's family chooses a woman, or the woman's family a husband, let them simply follow the ordinary principles of the Four Pillars and take the one whose chart is balanced, harmonious, level and smooth — that is all. What comes after the wedding, good or ill, may be left to Heaven.

Three things are being said here, and each of them governs a part of what follows.

The first is that there is no special art of matching. 只照四柱常理: only the ordinary principles of the Four Pillars. Whatever can be said about two people together is said with the same grammar that reads each alone — Day Master, season, strength, the Ten Gods, the Useful God, the relations among Stems and Branches. No table of years, no stars of ill omen, no separate machinery. This is why the next five chapters of a book about couples are about single charts.

The second is the criterion, and it has a name: 中和 (zhōng hé), central harmony. The phrase is not the fortune-tellers' own. It comes from the 中庸, where it is the condition under which heaven and earth keep their places and the ten thousand things are nourished. In the vocabulary of the first book it is balance — a chart whose Day Master can meet the demands placed on it, whose excesses have something to drain or restrain them and whose deficiencies have something to supply them. The classical critics do not ask whether two charts "attract." They ask first whether each chart, alone, is the chart of someone able to carry a marriage, and their measure of that is the chart's own equilibrium. Zhang Nan's two tests look lopsided — the woman examined for her husband and child stars, the man for his balance — and the lopsidedness is the old world speaking, in which a wife's fortune was her husband and her sons and a husband's was himself. Take the furniture away and the two tests are one test. Of each chart, ask what marriage is in it, and whether the self can bear it.

The third is the rule about heavy Companions and heavy Output, which is of a different kind from the other two, because it is not about balance but about imbalance. It says what to do with two charts neither of which is level. The miscellany at the end of 命理约言, as it has come down, makes the logic explicit by framing the rule with a question: if everyone picks only the well-fated to marry, what becomes of those whose charts are not? The answer is that they are matched to each other — excess to the excess that answers it. Why these two excesses in particular should answer each other is a question for Chapter Eight. What matters here is the principle, because nearly every living person has an imbalanced chart, and a method of matching that could only approve the balanced would be as useless as the one it replaced.

And then the last sentence. 婚后吉凶，听之于天可也. The men who wrote the most rigorous criticism of marriage divination that China produced did not conclude that a better divination would settle the matter. They concluded that a chart can tell a family whether it is giving its child to a person in reasonable balance, and that the rest is not the chart's to tell. Zhang Nan says the same from the other side: the lot of husband and wife is fixed from former lives, he grants, and yet failed marriages are also the fault of matchmakers who could not see; the rite of proposing a marriage is the beginning of the human way, and it must be done with care. Care, not certainty. That is the whole of what this art offers two people, and it is a good deal.

### The charts

Put the pair before the marketplace first, and see what it says.

He was born in a Dragon year and she in a Monkey year. Dragon and Monkey, 辰 and 申, are not among the six pairs of the jingle, nor among the six clashes; they are two of the three Branches of the Water frame, and the same lore that warns against the harms praises a match within a three-harmony above every other. By the second instrument the pair is approved at the door, before anyone has asked what month it was.

By the third instrument each is judged alone. His year, 丙辰, sounds as Earth — Sand Earth, in the old list — and Earth controls Water, so Water is his wife; Water's life stages run from Birth in the seventh month to Decline in the twelfth, and he was born in the fifth, where Water is an Embryo. He is therefore a man who drains the wealth of the family he marries into. Her year, 庚申, sounds as Wood — Pomegranate Wood — and Metal controls Wood, so Metal is her husband; Metal's stages run from Birth in the fourth month to Decline in the ninth, and she was born in the second, where Metal is an Embryo. She is a woman who drains the wealth of the family she enters. The two verdicts agree, for once, and they agree against both parties. Notice that they were reached without the charts being laid side by side at any point. Notice also what was used to reach them: for each person, the year and the month, and nothing of the day or the hour.

So the marketplace has produced, for the same two people, a blessing on the match and a pair of unrelated misfortunes, and it has not yet looked at the day on which either of them was born. This is what Zhang Nan meant by a doctrine with its head lopped off and its feet severed.

Now lay out what the critics demanded: all sixteen.

| | Year | Month | Day | Hour |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| His Stems | 丙 Yang Fire | 甲 Yang Wood | 壬 Yang Water | 癸 Yin Water |
| His Branches | 辰 Dragon | 午 Horse | 子 Rat | 卯 Rabbit |
| Her Stems | 庚 Yang Metal | 己 Yin Earth | 丙 Yang Fire | 庚 Yang Metal |
| Her Branches | 申 Monkey | 卯 Rabbit | 申 Monkey | 寅 Tiger |

And apply, roughly, Zhang Nan's two tests, since they are the first thing a classical reader would do. The woman's family examines the man for central harmony. They will not find it: the first book's whole diagnosis was that this Day Master is weak, born into the season that imprisons it, giving in three directions and replenished from none. The man's family examines the woman for her husband star and her child star. The child star they will find at once — it is her Output, and it stands in plain view on her Month Stem, with more of the same element hidden in three of her four Branches. The husband star they will find only if they know where to look: not on any Stem, and not in its proper form, but twice over in the irregular form, hidden in the Monkey of her Year and in the Monkey beneath her own Day Stem.

By the critics' own first standard, then, neither of these charts passes cleanly. He is not balanced, and she has her husband in the form the manuals distrusted and in a place they would have to dig for. A careless reader of Zhang Nan would send them both home, where the matchmaker had welcomed them, and for better reasons.

A careful reader would remember the second half of what he wrote. The rule about the heavy Companions and the heavy Output exists precisely for charts that fail the first test — and the first book's census of this man found three Rob Wealths, in his seat, in his Hour, and in the vault of his Year, while the Stem that stands exposed on this woman's Month is her Hurting Officer, with the Eating God hidden in three of her four Branches. Whether that is enough to bring the rule into play, and what the rule is really measuring when it does, must wait until each chart has been read for itself. Keep the marketplace's verdict in mind in the meantime. The rest of the book is the appeal — this time, against a verdict in the pair's favor, which is a stranger thing to appeal and will turn out to need it just as much.

---

## Chapter Three — The Spouse Palace

There are two ways to learn something about a guest who has not yet arrived. One is to ask who was invited. The other is to walk through the room that has been made ready. The second way tells you nothing about the guest's face or name, and it tells you a great deal that the first cannot: how much space has been left, what the light is like, whether the furniture is arranged for conversation or for ceremony, whether a window has been left open or the door has been built to stick. A chart offers both kinds of knowledge about a marriage. The star, which is the next chapter's subject, is the invitation. The palace is the room.

The tradition fixed its location early and never moved it. 渊海子平, in the passage quoted in Chapter One, makes the Day Stem the self and the Day Branch the wife. A rhapsody preserved in the same collection walks the whole chart in four phrases — the Year Stem the father and its Branch the mother, 日干己兮支妻, the Day Stem oneself and its Branch the wife, the Month for brothers, the Hour for children. A commentary carried in 神峰通考 states it as the definition of the method itself: 故子平之法，专以生日天元为主，日下支辰为妻宫 — the Ziping method takes the Stem of the birth day as its sole master, and the Branch beneath the day as the wife's palace. For a woman the same Branch is the husband's palace, 夫宫 (fū gōng); Zhang Nan uses the term without comment when he reads a woman's chart. The first book called it the spouse palace (夫妻宫 fū qī gōng), and that is the name this book keeps.

Why this Branch and no other is not a convention. The four pillars, read from Year to Hour, are four rings drawn tighter and tighter around the self: the lineage, the season and the working world, the self, the private hours and what comes after. The Stem of the day is the self at its most conscious. The Branch of the day is what that consciousness rests on — the first book's phrase was the seat of the self — and what a self rests on, daily and without thinking about it, is the life it shares with whoever shares it. No one is closer to the Day Stem than the character written directly beneath it. Chen Su'an, who rejected a good deal of what the old manuals said about family relations, kept the positions — the Year for the forebears, the Month for the parents, the Day Branch for the wife, the Hour for the children. This, he wrote, is a method with reason in it.

One distinction has to be held from the start, because the rest of the chapter depends on it, and it is this book's and not the manuals'. The old texts speak of the seat as the wife herself: seated on Wealth, she is worthy; seated on the Blade, she sustains her husband. This book reads the palace as a place, not a person. What sits in the Day Branch is not a portrait of the spouse; it is a description of the ground on which any spouse will be met — what the self expects of intimacy, what it brings into the room, what the room will do to whoever enters it. A man with a harsh Branch beneath him is not thereby promised a harsh wife. He is promised that the place where his marriage must live is a hard place, and that whoever joins him there will be joining him in that.

### What the palace holds

The first book read a seat by its element: a Day Master on its own element is rooted, on the element that generates it is fed from below, on the element that controls it is pressed in the place it should feel most at home, on the element it generates is giving even to its own foundation, on the element it controls is exercising authority over its most intimate ground. Every one of the sixty possible Day Pillars falls under one of those five, according to the principal Stem hidden in its Branch, and they fall evenly — twelve pillars to each.

| The seat holds, as its principal Stem | The Day Pillars |
|---|---|
| the self's own element | 甲寅 乙卯 丙午 丁巳 戊辰 戊戌 己丑 己未 庚申 辛酉 壬子 癸亥 |
| the element that generates the self | 甲子 乙亥 丙寅 丁卯 戊午 己巳 庚辰 庚戌 辛丑 辛未 壬申 癸酉 |
| the element the self generates | 甲午 乙巳 丙辰 丙戌 丁丑 丁未 戊申 己酉 庚子 辛亥 壬寅 癸卯 |
| the element the self controls | 甲辰 甲戌 乙丑 乙未 丙申 丁酉 戊子 己亥 庚寅 辛卯 壬午 癸巳 |
| the element that controls the self | 甲申 乙酉 丙子 丁亥 戊寅 己卯 庚午 辛巳 壬辰 壬戌 癸丑 癸未 |

Translate the rows into the Ten Gods and the table becomes a map of five kinds of marriage ground. In the first row the palace holds Companions: the closest partnership is with one's own kind, a mirror or a rival. In the second it holds Resource: the self expects, at home, to be fed. In the third it holds Output: home is where the self pours itself out. In the fourth it holds Wealth, and in the fifth, Authority — and here the table touches the next chapter, because Wealth is the wife star of a man and Authority is the husband star of a woman. The twelve pillars of the fourth row seat a man directly on his wife star; the twelve of the fifth seat a woman directly on her husband star. The tradition has a phrase for that arrangement, glossed in 神峰通考: 妻宫妻守, the wife keeping the wife's palace — the star at home in its own room — and the gloss promises such a man a wife to rival Meng Guang, the model of the devoted spouse.

A narrower fact hides inside the table. Because a Stem and its Branch must share a polarity, while four of the Branches carry a principal Stem of the polarity opposite to their own, the direct gods — the ones of opposite charge to the self — can be the principal Stem of the palace only on those four Branches: the Rat, the Horse, the Snake and the Pig. Only four of the sixty pillars seat a man on his Direct Wealth: 戊子, 己亥, 壬午, 癸巳. Only four seat a woman on her Direct Officer: 丙子, 丁亥, 庚午, 辛巳. Everyone else whose palace is headed by the spouse star has it there in its indirect form — Windfall for the man, the Merciless for the woman. That is not a defect. It is a description: for most people whose star lives in their palace, the marriage ground has an edge of the unconventional built into it.

And the principal Stem is not the whole household. Nine of the twelve Branches hold two or three Stems, and the lesser ones are also living in the palace. The Ox beneath a Day Master holds Earth, Water and Metal together; the Tiger holds Wood, Fire and Earth. A palace of that kind is a house with several rooms, and the reading must say who is in each. The three pure Branches — Rat, Rabbit, Rooster — are houses of one room. The reference's phrase for the Rat on the Day Branch is exact: what you get is what it is, without complexity but also without variety.

### The same contents, the opposite meaning

So far the palace has been read the way a beginner reads it, by asking which god lives there and whether that god has a pleasant name. Wealth and Officer in the palace sound fortunate; the Hurting Officer and the Blade sound like trouble. 渊海子平 says as much in its plain way: a man seated on Wealth or Officer under the day has a wife who helps him from within, and gains by her besides.

The passage that turns this from a list into a method is in 子平真诠, and it deserves to be read slowly.

> 以妻论之，坐下财官，妻当贤贵；然亦有坐财官而妻不利，逢伤刃而妻反吉者，何也？此盖月令用神，配成喜忌。
>
> To speak of the wife: seated on Wealth or Officer, the wife should be worthy and noble. Yet there are those seated on Wealth or Officer whose wives bring them no good, and those who meet Hurting Officer or Blade there whose wives are, on the contrary, a blessing. Why? Because it is the useful god of the Month Command that sorts what is welcome from what is not.

Shen Xiaozhan then gives four cases. The palace seated on Wealth is auspicious — but in a chart built on Resource, Wealth is the thing that breaks the structure, and there it is no blessing. The palace seated on Officer is auspicious — but a chart built on the Hurting Officer meets it there as an enemy. The palace seated on the Hurting Officer is inauspicious — but in a chart built on Wealth it feeds the Wealth, and in a chart built on Seven Killings it restrains the Killings, and in either the wife is a real helper. And the palace seated on the Blade is inauspicious — but:

> 妻坐阳刃，凶也，而或财官煞伤等格，四柱已成格局，而日干无气，全凭日刃帮身，则妻必能相夫。
>
> The wife's palace on the Blade is inauspicious; yet where the chart has formed its structure out of Wealth, Officer, Killings or Hurting Officer, and the Day Stem is without vigor and depends wholly on the Blade beneath the day to support the self, then the wife will certainly be one who sustains her husband.

His conclusion is a single clause: 其理不可执一, the principle must not be held to one reading.

In the first book's vocabulary this is simply the Useful God applied to one Branch. A palace is not good because of the name of what it holds. It is good if what it holds is something the chart needs, and it is troubled if what it holds is something the chart already has too much of. The Rival in the spouse palace of a Day Master drowning in Companions is one more mouth at an empty table. The Rival in the spouse palace of a Day Master who is weak and alone is the one ally who lives in the house. Same Stem, same Branch, opposite marriages. Chen Su'an puts the whole doctrine in a line: where an auspicious god dwells in the Day Branch, the person 主受妻室之力 — receives the strength of the spouse.

### The palace and its neighbours

A room is also defined by what it adjoins. The spouse palace stands between the Month Branch and the Hour Branch, with the Year Branch one remove away, and its relations with those three — harmony, clash, punishment, harm, as the first book and the reference set them out, and weighted by distance exactly as there — are the second thing a reader looks at.

A harmony binds the palace to the domain it joins. Bound to the Month, the marriage is entangled with the working life; the reference's reading of that pairing is that the partner is found through work, or the partnership is defined by a shared purpose. Bound to the Hour, partner and children form one intimate field. Bound to the Year, the spouse resembles or comes from the family's world. A bound palace is a steadied palace, with one caution that Chapter Nine will press: what is bound is also held, and a palace tied closely to something else has less of itself left over.

A clash is the relation the old texts speak of most, and most darkly. The same chapter of 子平真诠 that redeemed the Blade is blunt about this one. Even where the wife star is excellent and the palace useful, it says, 坐下刑冲，未免得美妻而难偕老 — if the seat is punished or clashed, one cannot avoid winning a fine wife and finding it hard to grow old with her. The rhapsodies in 渊海子平 keep returning to it. 时日对冲，伤妻克子: Hour and Day in mutual clash wound the wife and bear down on the children. 日时冲者，妻子招迟: where Day and Hour clash, wife and children are slow in coming. 妻宫有克，少年无早娶之人: where the wife's palace is struck, there is no early marriage in youth. Read together, the lines say something more exact than doom. A clashed palace is a room that cannot keep still. It delays the marriage, or moves it, or parts the couple for long stretches, or makes the peace of the house the thing that must be worked for every year instead of the thing that is simply there. The reference gives the two adjacent cases their domains: Month against Day, the career collides with the self and the partner with the vocation; Day against Hour, the partnership and the children, or the relationship and the creative legacy, cannot both thrive.

Two things temper a clash, and both come from the first book. One is the season, which decides who wins: a palace clashed by a Branch that the Month Command favors is under real attack, and a palace clashed by a Branch the season has abandoned is merely jostled. The other is what a clash opens. Four of the twelve Branches are vaults, and a vault in the spouse palace — Dragon, Dog, Ox or Goat beneath the Day Master — holds part of the marriage in storage, sealed. The old note to 滴天髓 says of the four vaults that they want to be clashed open, 宜冲开, and must not be shut; 三命通会 is explicit that for stored Wealth, Officer and Resource a clash is to be welcomed; and the common saying names punishment together with clash, 辰戌丑未，最喜刑冲. What shuts a vault, by the same note, is a combination, and the vaults 必不可闭, must never be shut. The first two texts of this canon counted a combination among the things that open a vault; on that one point of mechanism this canon follows the classical notes, and Chapter Nine sets the texts side by side. On the classical view a person with such a seat often finds that the marriage, or the best of it, arrives with a shock, and that a harmony on the seat keeps the shock from coming. The view of the opening clash had its doubters, and they were not minor ones. Shen Xiaozhan called the rule that nothing in a vault prospers until it is clashed a saying of the vulgar books, unknown to the founder of the method. A clash among the four vaults, he says, is Earth stirring Earth and not the collision of enemies: it is of use where Earth itself is the thing wanted, and where the thing wanted is the element in storage it only does that element harm. Ren Tieqiao called the rule simply mistaken.

Punishment and harm are quieter and wear longer. A punishment on the palace is a pattern that repeats — the same quarrel, the same overreach — and a harm is an erosion that works through a third party, each Branch spoiling the alliance the other was trying to make. Neither parts a couple the way a clash can. Both describe how a marriage is uncomfortable while it lasts.

And a palace that nothing touches — no harmony, no clash, no punishment, no harm from any of the three neighbouring Branches — is, in this book's image, a room with the door closed. It is stable, and it is waiting. Such charts are often those in which nothing about marriage seems to happen until a year or a Luck Pillar arrives carrying the one Branch that speaks to the seat.

### Marked seats

A few arrangements of Stem over Branch are distinctive enough that the tradition named them.

The first row of the table — the self seated on its own element — carries an old warning. 干与支同，损财伤妻, says one of the oldest pieces in 渊海子平, and the Four-Character rhymes repeat it: Stem and Branch alike, the wealth is lessened and the wife is hurt. The reasoning is the next chapter's: Companions are the natural rivals of the wife star, and a man who sits on his own element has a rival living in the room where his wife must live. Two pillars of that row go further, and a third joins them from the next. 丙午 and 壬子 seat a yang Day Master on its own element at the Branch of its Emperor's Peak, which is its Yang Blade; 戊午 does the same for Yang Earth, which keeps Fire's calendar, though the principal Stem of its Horse is its Resource. All three are the first book's 日坐羊刃, the Day Master seated on its Blade. These are the seats Shen Xiaozhan had in mind, and his ruling is the one to remember — ruinous for the strong, the saving of the weak.

Then there are the seats to which the Day Master is bound. Where the Stem that combines with the Day Master is hidden in its own Branch, the reference says, the attraction of the Five Combinations is present beneath the surface and the self is bound to what it sits on. It marks 丁亥, 戊子, 辛巳 and 壬午, where the partner is the principal Stem of the Branch, and 甲午 and 癸巳, where it is a lesser one; by the same logic the bond is present more faintly in 乙巳, 丙戌, 己亥, 庚辰, 戊辰 and 壬戌. 三命通会 knows the six that the reference marks by a name of its own — 座下自化, self-transformation beneath the seat — and thought well of most of them: 壬午 and 丁亥 the most deeply blessed, 戊子 clever, 辛巳 resourceful. Recall from Chapter One what the combining partner is. For a yang Day Master it is his Direct Wealth; for a yin Day Master it is her Direct Officer. So a man born on 戊子 or 壬午 has his wife star in his palace and is tied to it by the combination itself, and a woman born on 丁亥 or 辛巳 has her husband star in her palace on the same terms. No seat holds its marriage more tightly. The reference's caution about 戊子 applies to all of them: the spouse palace is held tightly, sometimes too tightly.

The last mark is an absence. The sixty-fold cycle runs in decades of ten pillars, and each decade leaves two of the twelve Branches unused; those two are that decade's Void (空亡 kōng wáng). When the Day Branch falls in the Void reckoned from the Year Pillar's decade, the old manual says 日坐空亡，难为妻妾 — seated on the Void, wife and concubine come hard. 三命通会 is more careful, and its care is worth more than the aphorism: the Void is empty and yet has substance, it says, and must not be taken straight off as ill; a Void that holds something harmful is a mercy, and ought not to be combined; and a Void that holds something good is mended by a combination, for what is combined can no longer be empty. The first two books of this canon do not use the Void, and this one will use it only as the tradition's best authors did, as a color and never as a verdict.

Two further marks belong to the Day Pillar as a whole and to the lore rather than to the method — the Lone Phoenix days and the days of Yin and Yang Mismatched. They are set out in Part Two with what the classical critics said of them.

### The stage beneath the self

One more layer comes from the Twelve Life Stages. The Branch beneath a Day Master is not only an element and a household of hidden Stems; it is a point on the Day Master's own cycle of birth, peak and burial, and the reference gives each of the twelve a reading for exactly this position. A self seated on its Birth meets a partner as renewal. On its Bathing the intimate self is naked — the stage of exposure, attraction and scandal, where the deepest intimacy and the deepest vulnerability are the same thing. On its Emperor's Peak, full power in the intimate space leaves no room for the partner. On its Tomb the self sits on a sealed vault and the partnership feels incomplete, not because something is missing but because something present has not been opened. On its Extinction there is a specific freedom: no expectations, no residue.

The life stage does not replace the reading by elements and gods. It tells where in the story the palace stands — whether the ground of the marriage is something beginning, something at its height, or something stored.

### The palace in time

Finally, the palace is not a fixed thing for seventy years. Every Luck Pillar and every year brings a Branch of its own, and that Branch stands in some relation to the Day Branch or in none. A Branch that clashes with the palace moves the room, a Branch that combines with it joins the room to something, and a Branch that repeats it doubles whatever the palace already was. What the old books make of each of these for the timing of a marriage is Chapter Ten's subject, and it is not what the handbooks say.

### The charts

His palace first, because the first book has already read most of it.

The Rat beneath Yang Water is one of the twelve seats of the first row, the self on its own element, and one of the Blade seats. The old warning applies in full: Stem and Branch alike, the wealth lessened and the wife hurt. Inside the Rat is a single Stem, Yin Water, the Rival — close enough, in the reference's words, to understand you completely, different enough to want what you have. A house of one room, and a competitor already living in it.

The beginner's reading stops there, with a difficult palace. Shen Xiaozhan's does not, and the case he describes might have been written for this chart. The structure is formed out of Wealth: the Month Command is the Horse, whose Fire is this Day Master's Wealth, with the Eating God above it feeding that Fire. The Day Stem is without vigor: Imprisoned by the season, the first book concluded, a weak Day Master. And what holds it up is the Blade beneath the day — the first book's own words were that for a Day Master short of support the Blade is the foundation that saves the chart. 全凭日刃帮身，则妻必能相夫. By the most rigorous classical statement on the subject, the Rival in this palace is not the wife's enemy. It is the strongest support the natal chart gives the self, and the marriage ground is therefore the most supportive ground this man has. The spouse palace holds his Auspicious God.

Then the neighbours. On one side the Horse, in the clash that defines the chart — and it is the Month–Day clash, at full strength, in summer, when the season arms the Horse. 坐下刑冲，未免得美妻而难偕老: the sentence stands over this seat exactly as it stands over the page. A palace that sustains him and cannot keep still; a fine wife, in the classical idiom, and the growing old together as the thing that will have to be earned. On the other side the Rabbit, in the one punishment that is a pair, the punishment of care that does not know its limits. Behind, at a remove, the Dragon, with the faint half of the Water frame. The first book read each of these for what it does to the self. Read for the marriage, they say that this man's home ground is assaulted by his work, overreached by his own tenderness, and distantly befriended by where he comes from.

The seat is also the Emperor's Peak of the Day Master, and the reference's sentence on that stage in this position was the first book's sharpest warning: the emperor has no equals, and the spouse palace demands one. And by the count from his Year Pillar the Rat falls in the Void. Hold that lightly, as instructed; it will matter once, later.

Her palace is another kind of place altogether, and a fuller one.

The Monkey beneath Yang Fire belongs to the fourth row: the self seated on the element it controls. Its principal Stem is Yang Metal, her Windfall. Beneath that, Yang Water, her Seven Killings — the husband star of her chart, in the only form her chart has it. Beneath that, Yang Earth, her Eating God, the Provider. Three rooms: one that holds what she governs, one in which the thing that governs her lives, and one through which she gives. The old rhapsody's line for a woman whose palace holds Wealth is 财旺生官，则夫纳福: where Wealth is strong and generates the Officer, the husband receives the blessing; and here the Wealth and the husband star are in one Branch, the one above the other, with the Provider beneath them both. The reference reads the Monkey on the Day Branch without knowing whose it is:

> The intimate foundation is sharp and resourceful. The spouse palace holds the Monkey's clever, adaptive energy — the closest partnership is one of intellectual stimulation, mutual problem-solving, and a restless need for variety. The body's ground is Yang Metal: the daily experience of being alive is quick, decisive, and impatient with dullness. The intimate self is cleverer than it lets on. The Monkey on the Day Branch means the partner encounters someone who is always working an angle, always seeing a solution, always one step ahead — which is exhilarating as a companion and exhausting as a life partner. The intimacy is stimulating but rarely still.

And the Stem on that ground:

> The sun melting raw ore. The Monkey's principal Geng Metal is the element Bing controls — Fire conquers Metal. The foundation is something the sun dominates: what lies beneath is being melted, refined, and reshaped by the heat from above. Hidden Water (Ren) inside the Monkey means the melting process also releases the element that controls Fire — the ore fights back by releasing water as it melts. The foundation is productive but oppositional: Bing can forge the Metal, but the Metal's hidden water resistance makes the process harder than it first appears.

Read that for the marriage and it says something exact. What her heat works on, in the intimate ground, gives back the very element that governs her. The husband star in this palace is not an intruder. It is what the ground yields when she works it.

Whether the Wealth at the head of her palace is welcome is Shen Xiaozhan's question, and his own sentence seems to answer it against her: 妻宫坐财，吉也，而印格逢之，反为不美 — the palace on Wealth is auspicious, but where the structure is formed out of Resource it meets it as unlovely. Her Month Command is the Rabbit, whose Yin Wood is her Direct Resource; hers is such a structure. But the Wealth in her palace does not stand alone. It stands over the Killings, and the Killings feed the Resource — the compendium's rule for her structure is 印绶逢煞则发, a Seal that meets the Killings flourishes, 印赖煞生, the Seal relies on the Killings for its life. Whether the palace is the Wealth that breaks a Seal or the relay that feeds one cannot be settled until her whole structure has been weighed, and that is Chapter Five's work. What can be said now is that the question turns on the husband star. It is the Stem in the middle.

Then the neighbours. Her Month Branch is the Rabbit, and Rabbit and Monkey stand in no named relation: her season leaves her room alone. Her Year Branch is the Monkey again, the same Branch as her palace — the family's ground and the marriage ground are one Branch, so that whatever her palace is, her origins were it first. A Song manual has a folk rule for a Year and a Day that fall on one position, and this book leaves it where it lies. Her Hour Branch is the Tiger, and the Tiger and the Monkey clash. It is the Day–Hour clash, adjacent, at full strength; and it is more than a clash, since Tiger and Monkey are also a leg of the punishment of ingratitude. The season arms the Tiger: in a Rabbit month Wood is Prosperous and Metal Imprisoned, and by the rule 旺者冲衰衰者拔 the Monkey is the party that is uprooted. The old note on the Branches has a further word for exactly this pair. Tiger, Monkey, Snake and Pig are the four places of birth, and 生方怕动: the places of birth dread being set in motion; Ren Tieqiao's gloss is 两败俱伤, both sides are hurt. His seat is struck by his season. Hers is struck by her own beginnings — the Hour, which is the palace of her children and her private world, and which also holds the Birth of her Day Master, her one root. The rhapsodies' lines for a Day–Hour clash are the ones given above: 时日对冲，伤妻克子, 日时冲者，妻子招迟 — for a woman, husband and children; and the reference's domain for the pairing is that the partnership and the children, or the relationship and what one makes in private, cannot both thrive. The punishment adds its own note: effort that goes unacknowledged between the room and the private world, favors that turn into grievances.

One thing the reference says of this clash should be kept beside the warning. What the collision of Tiger and Monkey liberates, it says, is the hidden Water in the Monkey and the hidden Fire in the Tiger. In her chart the hidden Water of the Monkey is her husband star, and the hidden Fire of the Tiger is her own root. The clash that unsettles her room is also what brings its husband star out of storage and sets her own Fire free. A struck palace of this kind does not keep its contents to itself.

Her Day Master's stage on the Monkey is Sickness. The reference: the spouse palace holds the energy of sickness — the closest partnership is one where something isn't functioning properly, where the dynamic between self and partner is weakened by a malfunction neither fully understands. That is the harshest sentence the reference has for any seat, and it should be weighed before it is believed. Fire in the Monkey is early autumn's Fire, the afternoon of the year, past its strength and not yet gone; and the stage is a statement about the Day Master's own vigor on this ground, not about the household in it. The three tenants of the palace are all of use to her structure, as the next chapters will show. The sun is weak here. What it sits on is not. By the count from her Year Pillar the Monkey does not fall in the Void; the two Branches her Year's decade leaves out are the Rat and the Ox, and the Rat is the Branch he sits on. Hold that lightly, as with his; it will matter later.

Set the two rooms side by side. His is a single chamber full of his own element, lively to the point of violence, struck from the side of his work and worn from the side of his private world, and holding the chief help his chart contains. Hers is a house of three rooms — what she governs, what governs her, and what she gives through — struck from the side of her children and her own beginnings, doubled by the ground she comes from, and left in peace by her season. Neither room can keep still. His is shaken by his season and hers by her Hour.

The Branches that would quiet a struck palace, the old note said, are the one in harmony with it, the two of its frame, the vault of its element, and a Noble. For his Rat the frame is the Monkey and the Dragon. For her Monkey the frame is the Rat and the Dragon. He was born sitting on the Rat, and his chart begins on the Dragon; she was born sitting on the Monkey, and her Year is a Monkey too. Each palace is a member of the frame that the other palace needs, and the third member of both frames stands at the head of his chart.

---

## Chapter Four — The Spouse Star

The palace is fixed. It is one Branch, and everyone has exactly one. The star is another matter. It may stand anywhere in the chart or nowhere; it may appear once, or four times, or in two forms at once; it may be the strongest thing in the chart or a trace buried in a storage Branch; it may be the element the Day Master most needs or the element that is ruining it. The palace says where a marriage lives. The star says what marriage is to this person — what kind of bond it is, how heavy, how single, how well the self can bear it — and no part of the reading is more variable or more easily got wrong.

### The two stars

The definitions are the oldest in the literature and were quoted in Chapter One's derivation. 渊海子平, on a man:

> 正财为正妻，偏财妾也。
>
> Direct Wealth is the principal wife; Indirect Wealth is the concubine.

and on a woman: 取官为夫，为福星 — take the Officer as the husband; it is her star of blessing — with the gloss that Seven Killings is the 偏夫, the husband on the side. For a man, then, the spouse star is the element his Day Master controls: Direct Wealth (正财) where the polarities differ, Indirect Wealth (偏财) where they are the same. For a woman it is the element that controls her Day Master: Direct Officer (正官) in the opposite polarity, Seven Killings (七杀) in the same.

| Day Master | A man's wife star | his secondary | A woman's husband star | her secondary | Combines with |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 甲 Yang Wood | 己 Yin Earth | 戊 Yang Earth | 辛 Yin Metal | 庚 Yang Metal | 己 |
| 乙 Yin Wood | 戊 Yang Earth | 己 Yin Earth | 庚 Yang Metal | 辛 Yin Metal | 庚 |
| 丙 Yang Fire | 辛 Yin Metal | 庚 Yang Metal | 癸 Yin Water | 壬 Yang Water | 辛 |
| 丁 Yin Fire | 庚 Yang Metal | 辛 Yin Metal | 壬 Yang Water | 癸 Yin Water | 壬 |
| 戊 Yang Earth | 癸 Yin Water | 壬 Yang Water | 乙 Yin Wood | 甲 Yang Wood | 癸 |
| 己 Yin Earth | 壬 Yang Water | 癸 Yin Water | 甲 Yang Wood | 乙 Yin Wood | 甲 |
| 庚 Yang Metal | 乙 Yin Wood | 甲 Yang Wood | 丁 Yin Fire | 丙 Yang Fire | 乙 |
