| 辛 Yin Metal | 甲 Yang Wood | 乙 Yin Wood | 丙 Yang Fire | 丁 Yin Fire | 丙 |
| 壬 Yang Water | 丁 Yin Fire | 丙 Yang Fire | 己 Yin Earth | 戊 Yang Earth | 丁 |
| 癸 Yin Water | 丙 Yang Fire | 丁 Yin Fire | 戊 Yang Earth | 己 Yin Earth | 戊 |

The last column repeats what the fable of the five weddings established, and it shows an asymmetry worth noticing. A yang Day Master combines with his Direct Wealth; a yin Day Master combines with her Direct Officer. So a man whose Day Master is yang has for his wife star the very Stem he is drawn to merge with, and a woman whose Day Master is yin has the same in her husband star. For a yin-Stem man and a yang-Stem woman the combining partner and the spouse star are different Stems: he is bound to his Officer and she to her Wealth, and the spouse star stands a little apart. 三命通会 preserves an older way of counting that tried to smooth this over — the wife is always the yin Stem of the controlled element and the husband always the yang Stem of the controlling one, sex following polarity — and then remarks, with some candor, that yang meeting yang is mostly without feeling, while yin meeting yang is husband and wife singing in accord. The reckoning by Direct and Indirect won, and it is the one this book uses. But the remark is true and the reader should keep it: where the spouse star is also the combining partner, the bond has a pull in it that the other half of humanity must build by other means.

The secondary stars need a word, because the names the tradition gave them belong to a vanished household. Indirect Wealth was the concubine and Seven Killings the irregular husband. Strip the institution and what remains is the first book's distinction between the direct and the indirect gods: the direct one is the socially legible, steady, harmonious form of a relation, and the indirect one is its intense, unconventional, less predictable form. A man whose only Wealth is Windfall has a wife star all the same; the classical authors are explicit that whichever is present, alone, serves. 三命通会 says it for women in so many words: with the Officer as husband one does not want to see the Killings, with the Killings as husband one does not want to see the Officer, and 一位为好 — one is best. What the indirect star describes is not a lesser spouse. It is a bond with more voltage in it and fewer rails.

One more thing is carried by these stars that the modern reader must be told plainly, because the classical authors never thought to explain it. Wealth is the wife, and Wealth is also wealth. 我去克他为妻财, says the rhapsody: what I go out and master is wife-and-property, one word. The Officer is the husband, and the Officer is also rank, law, the institution one answers to. The tradition did not think it was insulting anyone by this. It thought that a man's wife and a man's substance were the two things he had to be strong enough to keep, and that a woman's husband and a woman's standing arrived, in that world, as one event. For reading, the consequence is practical: nothing can happen to a man's wife star that is not also happening to his money and to everything else he takes responsibility for, and nothing can happen to a woman's husband star that is not also happening to her relation to authority and form. A year that robs the one robs the other. This is not a flaw in the system. It is one of its better observations about how a household actually works.

### Where the star stands

The first question about a star is where it is. 子平真诠 defines the term narrowly:

> 既看妻宫，又看妻星。妻星者，干头之财也。
>
> Having looked at the wife's palace, look also at the wife's star. The wife's star is the Wealth at the head of the Stems.

A star on a Stem is declared. It acts in the open, it is what the world sees of the marriage, and it is exposed — to combination with its neighbours, to being struck, to being contested. A star hidden in a Branch is present but private, and the first book's rule for all hidden Stems applies to it: a visible Stem is known, a hidden Stem is discovered, activated by combinations and by the years. Many people whose spouse star is hidden find that the marriage enters their life when a Luck Pillar or a year brings that Stem to the surface.

The pillar matters as it always does. A star at the Year stands at the outermost ring: early in life, or far away, or tied to the family's world, or simply at a distance from the self — the reference's rule for combinations is that Stems a pillar apart bond only weakly, and the same distance tells on every relation. A star at the Month stands in the arena of work and of the formative years, adjacent to the self and under the full authority of the season. A star in the Day Branch is the star at home in its palace. A star at the Hour stands in the private world and the later years: a marriage that comes late, or deepens late, or lives mostly out of public view.

### How strong, how many, how clean

Then the star is weighed like any other element, by the first book's instruments: the seasonal state, the roots, the life stage of the star on the Branch where it sits. The manuals say what follows from the weighing. 财命有气，妻妾和顺，是得妻力: where Wealth and self both have vigor, wife and household are in accord, and one has the strength of a wife. 财衰败墓绝，主妻有疾不贤: where it is in decline, bathing, tomb or extinction, the wife is ailing or unhelpful. For women, 三命通会 gives the whole requirement in a couplet that has not been bettered: 大抵夫星要值健旺，己身须禀中和 — in the main, the husband star should be found in health and vigor, and one's own self should be endowed with central harmony.

Number is the next question, and here more is not better. A star that appears once is a bond. A star that appears three times, or in both its forms at once, is a divided attention. Shen Xiaozhan on the man: where the wife star shows twice on the Stems, direct and indirect mixed, how can one husband have so many wives? — 亦防刑克之道也, this too is a road to injury. The old manual on the woman: of Killings and Officer, 只要一位者良, a single one is what is good; and the condition it most distrusts has a name, 官杀混杂 (guān shā hùn zá), Officer and Killings mingled. In a world of one spouse at a time the meaning is not a harem. It is a person for whom the relation itself comes in two voices that do not agree — the legitimate and the intense, the settled and the dangerous — and who is liable to be pulled between them, in sequence or at once.

A star has natural allies and natural enemies, and they are simply the generating and controlling cycles. Wealth is fed by Output: the man whose Eating God or Hurting Officer flows into his Wealth has a wife star with a source. Wealth is robbed by Companions: 比肩分夺, the manual says, shoulder-to-shoulder men dividing the spoil, and the rhapsodies never tire of it — 孤寡者，只为财神被劫, the solitary are solitary only because the Wealth god was plundered. The Officer is fed by Wealth, which is why the first line on women in 渊海子平 runs on to say 财旺生官，则夫纳福: where Wealth is vigorous and generates the Officer, the husband receives blessing. And the Officer is attacked by the Hurting Officer, the god named for exactly that. 伤官见官，为祸百端: the Hurting Officer meeting the Officer is calamity a hundred ways. Chapters Six and Seven will take each of these in its place; for now the point is only that a spouse star is never read alone. It is read with what feeds it and what strikes it.

Two conditions are special to a star because it is a Stem. It can be combined, and it can be buried.

A spouse star that combines with the Day Master is a star bound to the self, and that is the strongest attachment a chart can show. A spouse star that combines with some other Stem is a different thing. The reference's doctrine of the combinations applies without change: a Stem that is combined away is no longer fully available to play its original role. The old manuals put it in the terms of their world — where the Officer of a woman is combined by another Stem, there are maids and sisters contending for her place; where both a man's Wealth stars are found in combination, the household is disorderly. Read without the scandal, the observation is that the partner's attention, or the marriage's energy, is committed elsewhere: to a third person, sometimes; as often to a parent, a work, a cause. And where two Stems of the same kind reach for one star — two Companions of a woman both combining with her single Officer — the texts call it contested combination (争合 zhēng hé) and read it as rivalry outright. 三命通会 even rules on who prevails: the one whose own Stem is in vigor.

A star in its Tomb is a star in one of the four storage Branches, sealed. The rhapsodies are severe — 财星入墓，必定刑妻, a Wealth star entered into the tomb is a wife surely harmed — and then, in another place, they notice the other face of the same fact: 财星入库妻悭吝, a Wealth star in the storehouse is a thrifty wife who guards the purse. The first book's teaching on vaults reconciles them. What is stored is preserved and withheld at once. A spouse star in storage is a marriage that is late, or guarded, or held in reserve, and that the right year will bring out — by a clash, most of the old books say.

### When the star is missing

Some charts contain no spouse star at all, in any Stem or any Branch. The manuals have a name for it — 局中无妻, no wife within the configuration — and a rule: look then at where the day itself stands; if the Day Master is in a region where Wealth would flourish, there will be a capable wife all the same. For women the cruder texts are less patient. 四柱无夫，不偏房定为续室, says the long rhapsody in 三命通会: four pillars without a husband — if not a concubine, then certainly a second wife.

That sentence is the kind of thing that gave this art its bad name, and the tradition's better minds knew it. Chen Su'an answered it from observation:

> 人命常有无印而父母贵寿者矣，无食神伤官而子息繁昌者矣，无财官煞而夫妻安荣者矣。但就日主及全局观之，知其有无根气，则知其父母；知其有无生气，则知其子息；知其有无和气，则知其夫妻。
>
> One constantly meets charts with no Resource whose parents were honored and long-lived, charts with no Eating God or Hurting Officer whose children were many and flourishing, charts with no Wealth, Officer or Killings in which husband and wife were secure and esteemed. Look only at the Day Master and the configuration as a whole: know whether it has the breath of rootedness and you know the parents; know whether it has the breath of life and you know the children; know whether it has the breath of harmony and you know husband and wife.

The breath of harmony: 和气 (hé qì). It is the second half of 中和, and it is Chen's answer to the question of what a marriage is in a chart when the star that was supposed to stand for it is not there. The marriage is the chart's capacity for accord — whether its forces pass into one another or stand against one another. A star is one way that capacity shows. It is not the capacity.

### The star as a function

The deepest statement of this is in 滴天髓, and with it the tradition turned a corner. The verse on husband and wife:

> 夫妻因缘宿世来，喜神有意傍天财。
>
> The bond of husband and wife comes down from former lives; the Auspicious God, with intent, keeps close beside the heavenly Wealth.

The old note beneath it explains what the second line is doing. The wife is still read by the Wealth — but the Wealth is judged by its relation to what the chart needs. If the Auspicious God is itself the Wealth, the wife is fair, and rich, and honored. If the Auspicious God and the Wealth do not begrudge each other, that is also well. If they are at odds, 否则克妻，亦或不美，或欠和: then the wife is harmed, or she is not what was hoped, or harmony is wanting. And the note goes on to list what a Wealth star may itself require — support when it is thin, Companions when it is too strong for a weak self, an opening clash when it is shut in, a combination or a storehouse when it runs loose. The wife star has become a patient, with a diagnosis and a prescription of its own.

For women the old note goes further, and says something no earlier text had dared:

> 若官星太旺，以伤官为夫；官星太微，以财为夫；比肩旺而无官，以伤官为夫；伤官旺而无财官，以印为夫。
>
> If the Officer star is too strong, take the Hurting Officer as the husband; if the Officer star is too faint, take the Wealth as the husband; if the Companions are strong and there is no Officer, take the Hurting Officer as the husband; if the Hurting Officer is strong and there is neither Wealth nor Officer, take the Resource as the husband.

The Hurting Officer — the god the manuals had spent centuries calling the destroyer of husbands — named as the husband. The logic is the first book's eighth chapter exactly. An Officer too strong for the woman's chart is a disease, and the Hurting Officer is its medicine; a chart of strong Companions with no Officer has too much self and nowhere to send it, and the Hurting Officer is the outlet. In each case the "husband" is whatever god brings the chart into balance. Ren Tieqiao, commenting in the nineteenth century, removed the last hesitation: 凡女命之夫星，即是用神 — in every woman's chart the husband star simply is the Useful God — and added that one must not be fixed on the Officer as the husband.

The same thought, applied to men, appears in the seasonal handbook 穷通宝鉴 as a formula repeated month after month: using this element, that one is the wife and this one the child. Its rule is constant. The child is the Useful God; the wife is the element that generates the Useful God. A man's wife, on this view, is whatever feeds the thing his chart most needs.

So there are, by the end of the tradition, three answers to the question "where is the spouse in this chart," and a careful reading holds all three. By relation, the spouse is a star: Wealth for the man, Officer for the woman. By place, the spouse is a palace: the Day Branch. By function, the spouse is whatever brings the chart to accord. In a fortunate chart the three agree — the star sits in the palace and is also the element the chart needs — and that agreement is what the old phrase about the Auspicious God keeping close beside the Wealth describes. In most charts they do not agree, and the disagreement is not a contradiction to be explained away. It is the reading. It says that what this person pursues as marriage, where this person actually lives it, and what would do this person good are three different things, and it says which is which.

### The charts

His stars first. A Yang Water man has Yin Fire for his wife star and Yang Fire for its secondary, and the first book located both. Yin Fire, 丁, the Direct Wealth and his combining partner, is hidden: it is the principal Stem of the Horse, his Month Branch. Yang Fire, 丙, the Indirect Wealth, is visible, on the Year Stem.

Take Shen Xiaozhan's definition literally and the first finding is that this man's declared wife star — the Wealth at the head of the Stems — is the indirect one, standing at the outermost pillar of the chart, a pillar's width from the Day Master and in the Stem relation the first book called a clash, Yang Fire against Yang Water. The direct one is not declared at all. It lives underneath the working life, in the Branch of career and season. What shows of marriage in this chart is Windfall on the far horizon; what holds is Earned Fortune under the month. A rhapsody in 渊海子平 has a hard line for a chart whose Indirect Wealth is the one that appears — less love for the principal wife, more for the concubine. Take the vanished household out of it and the line still describes something: a man whose visible reaching is toward the bright and distant, and whose steady bond has to be looked for in the place where he works.

Now weigh the star. Fire, in a Horse month, is Prosperous; nothing in the chart is stronger. Yin Fire sits in the Branch that is its own Office. The Eating God stands directly above it, feeding it. And the self that must carry all this is the weak Day Master of the first book. The manuals have a phrase for the arrangement, and they agree about what it means for the marriage. 财多身弱，妻反胜夫, says 渊海子平: Wealth abundant and the self weak, the wife prevails over the husband. 男遇财多身弱，妻话偏听, says the rhapsody: a man of much Wealth and a weak self hears his wife's word before any other. It is not a prophecy of a domineering woman. It is the statement that in this chart the relation of marriage is heavier than the man who must hold it, and that he will tend to be held by it instead.

His star's enemies are there too: three Rob Wealths, in the seat, in the Hour, in the vault of the Year. 比肩分夺 — the plunderers of the wife star. And here the lesson of the last chapter repeats itself in a new place. The old note to 滴天髓 is precise: 财旺身弱，又喜比劫, where Wealth is vigorous and the self weak, Companions are on the contrary welcome. The same Stems that the aphorisms accuse of robbing the wife are, in this chart, the only reason there is a self here capable of having one.

Then the relation between star and palace. His wife star lives in the Horse. His palace is the Rat. The Branch that houses the star clashes the Branch that is the palace, and inside that clash the Day Master is drawn to the star by the hidden combination of 丁 and 壬. The first book called it one of the oldest marriage signatures in the tradition. The old note gives it a diagnosis in two characters. His Auspicious God is the Water in the Rat; his Wealth is the Fire in the Horse; they do not keep close beside each other with intent — they are at war across the two central pillars of the chart. 欠和: harmony is wanting. Not no marriage. A marriage in which the thing he reaches for and the ground he stands on do not agree.

And by function? Here two reckonings must be shown, because they do not quite agree. 穷通宝鉴's formula makes the wife the element that generates the Useful God. Its own prescription for Yang Water in the Horse month takes Yin Water as the Useful God, with Metal to assist — the same two elements the first book's diagnosis arrived at, in the handbook's own order — and on that prescription the wife is Metal. Apply the same formula to the first book's Useful God, which is Metal itself, and the wife moves one link up the chain: Earth, with Metal for the child. The two reckonings differ about which link to name. They agree about the chain, in which Earth bears Metal and Metal bears Water, and about the thing that matters here: by function this man's marriage does not lie in the Fire his star is made of. It lies up the supply line of his chart. By relation his wife is Fire, of which he has too much. By place she is Water, of which his palace is made. By function she is Metal, or the Earth that bears it — and the first book's census of Metal in this chart, visible and hidden and stored, was none.

Her stars. A Yang Fire woman has Yin Water for her husband star proper and Yang Water for its secondary, the Seven Killings. Chapter One has already searched for them. There is no 癸 anywhere in her eight characters. There is 壬 twice, and both times it is hidden: the secondary Stem of the Monkey at her Year, and the secondary Stem of the Monkey beneath her own Day Stem.

Take Shen Xiaozhan's definition literally — the star is the Stem on the heads — and she has no husband star declared. Take the whole chart, as he also asks, and she has one in two places, in one form only, and one of the two places is her spouse palace. The crude verdict on a husband star found only as the Killings is on the page above: 偏夫, the irregular husband. Zhang Nan's correction has been quoted and it decides the matter: where there is no Direct Officer, the Seven Killings is the husband proper. And the manuals' graver fear, the mingling of Officer and Killings, does not touch her. Her husband star appears in one voice. 只要一位者良.

Now weigh the star. Water, in a Rabbit month, is at Rest: past the season that empowered it, not yet in the one that imprisons it. In both of the Branches that hold it, Yang Water stands at its own Birth; the Monkey is the spring from which Water first rises, and her chart has two of them. The star is young and rooted twice over. What feeds it stands directly above it, in the same Branch and on two Stems besides: Yang Metal, her Windfall, four times in the chart — the Monkey's principal Stem twice, the Year Stem and the Hour Stem. 财滋杀: Wealth nourishes the Killings. What restrains it stands directly beneath it, in the same Branch and on the Month Stem: Yang Earth, the Eating God, three times hidden, and Yin Earth, the Hurting Officer, declared. 食神制杀: the Provider governs the Killings. So her husband star stands between its feeders and its governors, four of one and four of the other, with two of itself. The old lines about the Killings demand exactly this balance, and warn against each excess: a Killings with no restraint is a brute, and one restrained too far, 制伏太过, is a poor scholar. Hers is held on both sides.

Then the relation between star and palace. In his chart the star lived in the Horse and the palace was the Rat, and the two Branches clashed. In hers the star lives in the palace. The Monkey beneath her Day Stem holds her Wealth at its head, her husband star beneath it, and her Provider beneath that: the whole economy of a marriage in one Branch, in the order the tradition likes — Wealth feeding the husband, the husband governed from below. The compendium's phrase for a man's wife star in the wife's palace was 妻宫妻守, and it promised a Meng Guang. The woman's counterpart is on the fifth row of Chapter Three's table, where the husband star is the principal of the palace; hers is on the fourth, where the Wealth that feeds him is the principal and he is the second tenant. The husband keeps the husband's palace, but as the one who is kept.

And by function? Here, for once, the two reckonings agree with each other and with the relation. 穷通宝鉴's prescription for Yang Fire in the second month is in four characters, 耑用壬水 — use Yang Water alone; the sun of spring wants the ocean to be seen in. The handbook then names its conditions for the highest grade: 壬 declared on a Stem, no 丁 to combine it away, 庚, 辛 and 己 declared as well, and 壬 rooted. Her chart meets them in part. Of the three Stems the handbook wants declared beside the Water, Yang Metal stands on two of hers and Yin Earth on a third, and the Yin Metal is absent; there is no Yin Fire anywhere; and her 壬 is rooted twice. What is missing is the one Metal, and above all the declaration itself, since her Water is hidden. More than that: the handbook gives two model charts for a Yang Fire of this month, and both are 丙申 days, and the first of them stands in a 己卯 month. Her Month Pillar and her Day Pillar are the handbook's own example to the character; where she differs from it is in the Year and the Hour. Ren Tieqiao's rule, that the husband star is the Useful God, therefore returns the same Stem the fable's rule returned. The handbook's own formula for a woman — take its wife for the husband — would return Metal instead, and Chapter Seven weighs that; this book follows Ren.

So her chart has a husband star, in the form the old books distrusted, in the room where a husband belongs, fed and governed in equal measure, and it is the same Stem by every reading the tradition has: by relation, by place, and by function. He is made of Yang Water.

Set the two findings beside each other.

His chart, asked by function where its marriage lies, answers Metal, or the Earth beneath it. Her Day Master is Fire. But four of her fourteen characters are Metal, two of them declared on her Stems, and four more are the Earth that bears it. What his chart points to is not what she is. It is what she carries.

Her chart, asked who her husband is, answers Yang Water by every road. His Day Master is Yang Water.

By the fable of the five weddings these two are each other's spouse stars in person, in the charge the fable does not marry, and Chapter One said so. By the functional reading — the latest the tradition reached, and one school's — he stands where her chart points, and she stands beside where his does, with the thing it wants in her hands. That is one finding, and a single finding is not a reading. It is the first unequal thing the book has found between them, and Chapter Eight will weigh it.

---

## Chapter Five — Star and Palace Together

A physician who takes the pulse and then looks at the tongue does not hand the patient two reports. The pulse is read in the light of the tongue and the tongue in the light of the pulse, and both in the light of what the season is doing to everyone's body that month; the diagnosis is the one thing all three say when they are heard together. The last two chapters took the palace and the star separately because each has its own vocabulary. They are not separate in a chart. A reading that announces "a good palace and a difficult star" and stops has not yet begun.

The instruction to join them is Shen Xiaozhan's — 既看妻宫，又看妻星, having looked at the wife's palace, look also at the wife's star — and it is followed in 子平真诠 by four sentences that are the nearest thing the classical literature has to a worked method. This chapter is a commentary on them.

### Four sentences

> 妻透而成局，若官格透财、印多逢财、食伤透财为用之类，即坐下无用，亦主内助。
>
> Where the wife star stands revealed and completes the configuration — as when a chart built on the Officer shows Wealth, or one heavy with Resource meets Wealth, or a chart built on Output shows Wealth and puts it to use — then even if what lies beneath the seat is of no use, she is still an inner help.

> 妻透而破格，若印轻财露、食神伤官透煞逢财之类，即坐下有用，亦防刑克。
>
> Where the wife star stands revealed and breaks the structure — as when Resource is slight and Wealth is exposed, or Output shows the Killings and then meets Wealth — then even if what lies beneath the seat is useful, guard against injury all the same.

> 又有妻透成格，或妻宫有用而坐下刑冲，未免得美妻而难偕老。
>
> And there is the case where the wife star completes the structure, or the wife's palace is useful, and yet the seat is punished or clashed: then one cannot avoid winning a fine wife and finding it hard to grow old with her.

> 又若妻星两透，偏正杂出，何一夫而多妻？亦防刑克之道也。
>
> And where the wife star is revealed twice, direct and indirect appearing mixed — how should one husband have so many wives? This too is a road to injury.

Three judgments are being ranked here, and the ranking is the lesson.

The first two sentences decide between the star and the palace when they disagree, and the star wins — but not the star as an element with a pleasant name. The star as a part of the structure. A wife star that the chart needs makes the marriage a help even over an indifferent palace; a wife star that damages the chart makes the marriage a danger even over a useful one. 内助 (nèi zhù), inner help, is the tradition's highest word for a spouse, and what earns it is not being Wealth. It is being Wealth in a chart that wanted Wealth.

The third sentence then sets a limit on both. Whatever the star and the palace's contents promise, the peace of the seat decides whether the promise can be kept. A good star and a useful palace, clashed, give the fine wife and the hard growing-old. The star, read through the structure, says whether the spouse is a help. The seat's relations with its neighbours say whether the help can be held onto. They are different questions, and a chart can answer yes to the first and no to the second — which is the most poignant marriage signature there is, and far from the rarest.

The fourth sentence is about singleness, and Chapter Four has covered it.

### Structure, in the first book's words

Shen Xiaozhan writes in a dialect the first book did not use, and one word in it is a trap. He takes the god that the Month Command reveals as the keynote of the chart and calls it the chart's 用神 — the very word the first book translated as Useful God. He calls the resulting configuration its 格局 (gé jú), its structure, and judges every other god by whether it completes that structure or breaks it. The first book, following the line of 滴天髓, meant something else by the same word: the single element that would correct the chart's central imbalance. These are two concepts under one name, and they do not always point at the same god. For marriage reading they come to the same instruction. Before saying whether a star or a palace is good, find out what the chart is trying to be and what it needs in order to be it. Then ask of the spouse star, and of what lives in the spouse palace: is this on the side of what makes the chart work, or against it?

Ren Tieqiao gives the same teaching a pair of names that the reader may find easier to carry:

> 夫财以妻论，财星清，则中馈贤能；财神浊，则河东狮吼。清者，喜神即是财星，不争不妒是也。浊者，生煞坏印，争妒无情是也。
>
> Wealth is read as the wife. Where the Wealth star is clear, the mistress of the house is worthy and capable; where the Wealth god is turbid, it is the lioness roaring east of the river. Clear means: the Auspicious God is itself the Wealth star, and there is no contention and no jealousy. Turbid means: it breeds the Killings and ruins the Resource — contention, jealousy, no feeling.

Clear and turbid (清浊 qīng zhuó). A clear spouse star is one whose presence costs the chart nothing: it is what the chart wanted, and nothing has to fight it or be sacrificed to it. A turbid one arrives entangled — feeding what attacks the self, wrecking what supports it, contested by rivals. The lioness roaring east of the river is the old joke about the henpecked scholar, and Ren uses it to make a serious point. The quarrelsome marriage is not caused by a bad wife. It is caused by a wife star that the husband's chart cannot receive cleanly.

And in the same passage he corrects, in a sentence, a thousand years of careless reading:

> 旧书不管日主之衰旺，总以阳刃劫财主克妻，究其理则实非，须分日主衰旺喜忌之别，四柱配合活看为是。
>
> The old books, without regard to whether the Day Master is strong or weak, always make Blade and Rob Wealth the signs of a wife overcome. Examine the principle and it is simply not so. One must distinguish the Day Master's strength and weakness, what it welcomes and what it shuns, and read the four pillars in their combination as a living thing.

### Can the self carry it

The second half of the synthesis is the one the manuals reduce to slogans, and the slogans are worth learning because each names a real condition.

A spouse star is a weight. For a man it is the element he controls, and control spends energy; for a woman it is the element that controls her, and being held to form spends energy too. The first question after "is the star welcome" is therefore "is the self strong enough to bear it" — and here the tradition is entirely symmetrical, whatever its social prejudices.

For a man, the star too heavy for the self is 财多身弱 (cái duō shēn ruò), Wealth abundant and the self weak. 渊海子平 has the proverb for it: 财多身弱，正为富屋贫人 — the poor man in the rich house, surrounded by what he cannot use. The wife prevails over the husband; her word is heard first; and what he needs is not less of a wife but more of himself — Companions and Resource, in the chart or in the years. The opposite fault is the self too heavy for the star: Wealth thin, the Day Master strong, and Companions crowding. The rhapsody names both of the classical roads to an injured marriage in one line:

> 伤妻叠叠，财轻身旺弟兄多；若不如斯，定是刑冲妻妾位。
>
> Wife after wife hurt: the Wealth is light, the self strong, the brothers many. If it is not that, then surely the wife's position is punished or clashed.

Star robbed, or palace struck. There is the whole chapter in two lines. And between the two faults lies the condition Chen Su'an calls 财与命相停, Wealth and self evenly matched, of which he says simply: 乃为和谐, that is harmony.

For a woman the same two faults have the opposite names. The star too heavy for the self is the Officer and Killings in strength over a weak Day Master. Zhang Nan, who is otherwise hard on women's charts, sees this one clearly: a husband star too vigorous, doubled and tripled over a weak self, is no good husband, he says, and what the chart then wants is Output to restrain it — and not too much of that either: 若制之太过，则又不足，贵得中和, for if the restraint is overdone there is a new deficiency; what is prized is to arrive at central harmony. The opposite fault is the self too strong for the star. 日主旺相，夺夫权而孤苦, says the long rhapsody: the Day Master in vigor seizes the husband's authority and is left solitary. Ren Tieqiao: 不敬丈夫，皆因官弱身强 — want of respect for a husband comes always from an Officer that is weak and a self that is strong.

The modern reader will hear the household law again in those last two lines, and should. But take away the question of who ought to defer to whom and the observation stands for both sexes alike. A marriage is a relation of mutual shaping. Where one party's chart has far more self than its spouse star can engage, that person tends to live in the marriage alone — unshaped, unmet, running the house and resenting it. Where the spouse star far outweighs the self, that person tends to disappear into the marriage. The first is the classical "seizing the husband's authority" and the classical "plundering of the wife star." The second is the poor man in the rich house, and the wife whose husband star, doubled and tripled over her, has become what Zhang Nan flatly calls no good husband. The tradition's ideal is neither. It is the couplet from Chapter Four, and it applies to everyone: the spouse star in health and vigor, the self endowed with central harmony.

### Disease and medicine

Zhang Nan's lasting contribution to the art was a single change of attitude, and it bears on marriage more than on anything else. He had himself judged charts by central harmony, he says, before he understood this, and had been right once or twice in ten. He quotes the old verse:

> 有病方为贵，无伤不是奇；格中如去病，财禄两相随。
>
> Only what has a sickness can be noble; what has no wound is nothing rare. If within the pattern the sickness is removed, wealth and rank follow together.

A chart is not judged by whether it has a fault. Every chart has one; the first book said the tilt is the chart's character. Nor does the verse quarrel with the ideal of central harmony, as it is sometimes taken to. The old note to 滴天髓 settles the matter in a breath: the first couplet speaks of the tilt, the second of the tilt put right, and a sickness removed is central harmony again. A chart is judged by whether the fault has a remedy — 病药 (bìng yào), disease and medicine — and by where the remedy is to be found: in the chart itself, or in the years, or nowhere.

Apply that to a marriage. The disease is whatever injures the star or disturbs the palace: the Companions that rob a man's Wealth, the Hurting Officer that strikes a woman's Officer, the clash on the seat, the star too heavy or too light. The medicine is whatever answers it, and the generating and controlling cycles say what can. Often it is a bridge. Companions attack Wealth; but Companions generate Output and Output generates Wealth, so a chart with Output standing between them turns the rivals into suppliers. The Hurting Officer attacks the Officer; but the Hurting Officer generates Wealth and Wealth generates the Officer, so a woman's chart with Wealth between the two has the quarrel converted into a relay — the verse in the old manual says exactly this: 女命伤官格内嫌，带财带印福方坚, the Hurting Officer in a woman's chart is disliked, but let it carry Wealth, let it carry Resource, and the blessing is firm. Sometimes the medicine is a restraint: Resource to hold an overflowing Output, Authority to hold overflowing Companions. Sometimes it is a combination that ties up the offending Stem, or a harmony that stills a clashing Branch.

Three places a remedy can be found; and here, at last, is the reason this book has spent five chapters on single charts. A disease whose medicine is in the natal chart is a difficulty already answered. A disease whose medicine arrives with a Luck Pillar is a difficulty with a season. And a disease whose medicine is in neither — that is where a second chart can matter most. Not because the other person "fixes" anyone. Because a person is, among other things, a standing configuration of elements, and to live beside one is to live beside what that configuration contains.

### What the tradition thought a good marriage looked like

Among the specimen charts of women in 三命通会 there is one given under a heading that is not a judgment of virtue or vice but simply a description of a life, and it is the tradition's portrait of the thing itself:

> 安静守分，乃夫星有气，日干自旺，相停无克，不值刑冲，财食得所者是也。
>
> Quiet, and keeping to one's part: this is where the husband star has vigor and the Day Stem is itself strong, the two evenly matched with neither overcoming the other, no punishment or clash encountered, and Wealth and Eating God each in its proper place.

The example chart is read through, and then the summary: 此乃自己夫星两不相伤，各乘旺气，无混乱相侵，夫妇偕和 — oneself and the husband star do each other no injury, each rides its own vigor, nothing is mixed and nothing encroaches, and husband and wife are in accord together.

Five conditions, and every one of them is something this book has now given the reader the means to check. The star has vigor: Chapter Four. The self has vigor: the first book. The two are evenly matched, neither crushing the other: this chapter. No punishment or clash on the seat: Chapter Three. And the gods that feed the star and relieve the self are present and unharmed. It is not an exciting chart. The heading says so. It is a chart in which the relation of marriage and the person who must live it are the same size, and the room they share is left in peace.

### The order of the questions

For one chart, then, the reading goes in this order, and the order matters. First the first book's diagnosis — the Day Master's strength, the chart's need — because nothing about the marriage can be weighed without it. Then the palace: what it holds, and whether what it holds is welcome. Then the palace's neighbours: whether the seat is bound, struck, worn, or left alone. Then the star: where it stands, how strong it is, whether it is single, what feeds it and what attacks it, whether it is combined or buried or absent, and if absent, what does its work. Then the weighing of star against self. Then the relation between the star and the palace themselves — whether the star lives in the palace, or in a Branch that harmonizes with it, or in a Branch that strikes it. And last the diagnosis in Zhang Nan's terms: what is the disease of this marriage, and where, if anywhere, is its medicine. Time comes after all of that, and the other chart after time.

### The charts

His chart has been waiting for those four sentences since the first book, and it answers to the third of them almost word for word.

The structure is built on Wealth: Fire commands the month, the Eating God stands over it and feeds it, and Fire shows on the Year Stem. In Shen's own terms such a structure wants a strong self to complete it, and this self is not strong, so the first sentence fits him only by half: the wife star is revealed and belongs to the structure, and the structure is more than the man. It is the second limb of the third sentence that fits him whole. The seat is useful. It is the Blade under a Day Stem without vigor, which Chapter Three found to be the chief support in the chart. And then the seat is clashed: 妻宫有用而坐下刑冲. By Shen Xiaozhan's own formula this is the man who wins a fine wife and finds it hard to grow old with her — hard, not impossible; the word in the text is 难.

Weigh the star against the self and the second difficulty appears. This is the poor man in the rich house. Wealth in season, fed, doubled; the Day Master Imprisoned, giving in three directions. He does not have too little marriage in his chart. He has more than he can lift. Ren Tieqiao's categories say the same: this is not a clear Wealth star, since the Auspicious God is not the Wealth but its opponent; it is Wealth that the self welcomes as a man welcomes a guest he cannot afford to feed.

So the diagnosis has two parts. The disease of the palace is that it cannot be still: the Horse strikes it, and in the Horse's own season. The disease of the star is its weight. The medicine for the second is plain and the first book named it — whatever strengthens the self, which is Metal first and Water after. The medicine for the first would have to be something that quiets a clash, and nothing in the natal chart does so with any strength: the Dragon at his Year, which belongs to the Rat's frame and is the vault of its element, stands too far off, and the Rabbit at his Hour, which is one of his two Nobles and so one of the four stillers the old note names, stands in punishment with the very seat it would protect. He is, in Zhang Nan's sense, a man with a sickness worth having: clearly defined, and with a remedy that exists. It is simply not inside his own eight characters.

Her chart requires the full procedure, since no earlier book has read it.

The Day Master is Yang Fire, born in the Rabbit month, the middle of spring. The decree of that month is Wood Prosperous, Fire in Mutual Generation, Water at Rest, Metal Imprisoned, Earth Dead. The Day Master is not in command of its season, but the season is on its side: Fire is the child of the month, the second of the five states. The Month Command is Yin Wood, her Direct Resource; hers is a Resource structure, the Seal in command of the chart. For root she has two positions of four. The Tiger beneath her Hour holds Yang Fire as its secondary Stem, her own element at the stage of its Birth; the Rabbit beneath her Month holds Yin Wood, the element that generates her. Both are in season. For surface allies she has none. The three Stems that stand beside her are all on the other side of the ledger: Yang Metal, her Windfall, at the Year and again at the Hour, and Yin Earth, her Hurting Officer, at the Month. Then the balance. The supporting camp is Fire and Wood, four presences, all seasonally empowered. The challenging camp is Earth four, Metal four and Water two, ten presences, all seasonally weak: Earth Dead, Metal Imprisoned, Water at Rest. And the flow. It runs out of her in two directions at once, through the Hurting Officer into Earth and through Earth into the Metal of her Wealth, and it comes back by a thin channel: the two Waters hidden in the Monkeys feed the two Woods, and the Woods feed her. Every element is in the chart and the cycle runs all the way round. It is simply wider going out than coming back.

The diagnosis is a weak Day Master, and a less weak one than his. He is Imprisoned by his season and she is favored by hers; he has one companion on the surface and she has none; he has four supports in eleven and she has four in fourteen. Both are selves with less than their charts demand of them. Hers is a self that gives more than it is given, in a season that keeps giving it a little.

The Useful God, by the first book's method, follows from that. A weak Day Master wants its own element and the element that generates it, and here the order matters. Four Metals stand against two Woods, and Metal controls Wood: the oldest hazard of a Resource structure, 贪财坏印, greed for Wealth ruining the Seal, is written across her chart in the numbers. Fire controls Metal and protects the Seal, and Fire is herself; so by the method the Useful God is Fire, and the Auspicious God is Wood. 穷通宝鉴 says something different, and Chapter Four quoted it: for Yang Fire in the second month, 耑用壬水, Yang Water alone. The two prescriptions are not enemies, and an old note to 滴天髓 says why. Where Fire is the Day Master and Metal and Wood are both present, it says, Wealth and Seal cross each other — 财印交差 — and 必须有官煞以和之: there must be Officer or Killings to harmonize them, and where the chart has none, the luck must bring them. The handbook's Water is the god that stands between her Metal and her Wood and turns the one into the other: Wealth feeding the Killings, the Killings feeding the Seal, the Seal feeding her. Her chart has that god, twice, hidden, and has it thinly. The book will use the first book's method for her strength and the handbook's prescription for her circulation, and will say which it is using where.

Now the palace, by Shen Xiaozhan's test. The Monkey's principal Stem is her Windfall, and Shen's own sentence was that the palace on Wealth is unlovely in a Resource structure; the Wealth at the head of her room is the very thing that threatens her Seal. But the tenant beneath it is the Killings, and the compendium's rule for her structure is 印绶逢煞则发 — the Seal that meets the Killings flourishes. The Metal at the head of the palace feeds the Water beneath it, and that Water feeds the Wood that commands her chart. The room holds the hazard and the remedy in one Branch, the hazard on top; the Eating God at the bottom keeps the remedy from growing into a hazard of its own. By the test of Chapter Three the head tenant is not welcome and the second tenant is what makes him bearable. The Water in her palace is the mediating god the old note asked for, standing exactly where it is needed and in half the strength the chart would like.

The star, as Chapter Four found, is that same Water, and it can now be weighed against the self. Water at Rest; Fire in Mutual Generation. Two of the one, two of the other. The husband star is rooted twice at its Birth and fed by four Metals; the self is rooted twice and fed by two Woods. 相停: evenly matched, which Chen Su'an called harmony and the compendium made the second condition of its portrait of a quiet marriage. Run that portrait's five conditions down her chart. The husband star has vigor: yes. The Day Stem has vigor: in season, not in strength. The two are evenly matched: yes. Wealth and Eating God are each in their place: abundantly. No punishment or clash is met on the seat: no. The Tiger strikes the Monkey, and it strikes it with the season behind it.

So the diagnosis has three parts, and the first is the one she shares with him. The disease of the palace is that it cannot be still; and where his room is struck by his season, hers is struck by her Hour, by the Branch that is at once her children's palace, her private world and her own root. Shen's third sentence was written for a man, and its logic is hers: a useful palace, a star that belongs to the structure, and a seat that is clashed. The second part is the star's condition. It is real, rooted, fed and governed, and it is never shown; the one condition of the handbook's highest grade that her chart does not meet is a Yang Water in the open. The third is the crossing of her Wealth and her Seal, four against two, with the mediator between them at half strength. The medicine for the first would be one of the four things the old note names for a struck Branch: the Snake, which combines with the Monkey; the Rat or the Dragon, which gather with it; the vault of its frame's element, which is the Dragon again; or a Noble. The medicine for the second and third is one thing: Yang Water declared and strong, which would be at once her husband star in the open and the god that turns her Metal into her Wood. None of this is in her own eight characters. The Rat and the Dragon are in his, and the first Stem of his Day Pillar is Yang Water.

Two charts, two diseases, and they rhyme. His marriage ground is struck by his work and his star is more than he can carry. Hers is struck by her private world and her star is kept where no one sees it. He needs something that stills his room. So does she. Neither chart holds the remedy for its own seat. It is time to see how a man's chart and a woman's chart each raise these questions in their own idiom, and then to put the two side by side.

---

## Chapter Six — The Man's Chart

The classical manuals have no chapter on the charts of men. They have chapters on charts, and then, near the end, a chapter on the charts of women. The reader the authors had in mind was a man asking about a man, and everything they wrote about the self, its strength, its career and its fortune assumes him. What they say of his marriage is therefore not gathered in one place. It is scattered through the chapters on Wealth, through the lists of kin, and above all through the rhapsodies, where a line about a wife turns up between a line about an inheritance and a line about a prison.

This chapter gathers it. A man's chart was asked four questions about marriage, and the old phrases for them are worth having. 得妻力 (dé qī lì): does he have the strength of a wife — is his marriage a help to his life or a charge upon it? 贤淑 (xián shū), worthy and gentle: what is she like? 早娶 (zǎo qǔ), marrying early: or does he marry late? And 刑克 (xíng kè), punishment and overcoming: does the marriage hold, or is there more than one? The answers all turn on a small number of weights, and by now the reader knows what they are.

### The neighbours of the wife star

Chapter Four said that a spouse star is never read alone. For a man that sentence can be made exact, because each of the other four families of gods stands in a fixed relation to his Wealth, and each has a helpful face and a harmful one.

The Companions are the wife star's rivals. Wealth is what the Day Master controls, and whatever shares the Day Master's element controls the same thing and reaches for it. The old lines on this are the most quoted in the literature and the most misused. 男多阳刃，必重婚: a man with many Blades will certainly marry twice. 比肩成局，几度新郎: Companions formed into a frame — a bridegroom how many times over. Zhang Nan's verse on the second is blunter still: 我成我局必伤妻，比占妻宫总不宜 — where I form my own frame the wife must be hurt, and Companions occupying the wife's palace are never right. A marriage, on this view, is a seat at a table, and a chart full of the self's own kind has too many men at it.

That is half of the truth, and Chapter Five has already quoted the sentence in which Ren Tieqiao supplied the other half: the old books make Blade and Rob Wealth signs of a wife overcome without asking whether the Day Master is strong or weak, and on examination it is simply not so. Where the Wealth is heavy and the self is light, Companions are the only thing that lets a man hold a marriage at all — 财旺身弱，又喜比劫, in the old note's words. The line about many Blades is true of a strong man with thin Wealth. Of a weak man with heavy Wealth it is the reverse of true.

Output is the wife star's source. The element the Day Master generates is the element that generates his Wealth, so a man whose Eating God or Hurting Officer stands near his Wealth and flows into it has a wife star that is fed, and fed by his own effort and talent: what he makes sustains what he keeps. Output does a second service that matters even more for this book. It stands between the Companions and the Wealth. Companions attack Wealth, but Companions generate Output, and Output generates Wealth; a chart with Output in the middle has turned its robbers into suppliers. Remember this bridge. It is the natal form of the one rule Zhang Nan gave for matching two charts, and Chapter Eight will find it there again.

Authority is the wife star's child and its guard. Wealth generates the Officer, and a chart where vigorous Wealth feeds a clean Officer is the manuals' picture of the fortunate household — 财旺生官，夫唱妇随, the husband leads the song and the wife takes it up: in the old world's terms, the wife whose management of the house becomes the husband's standing outside it. The Officer also restrains the Companions, which is why Ren Tieqiao's first case of an injured marriage has three conditions and not two: Wealth light, Companions many, and no Officer to keep them off. But Authority draws on Wealth to live. Where the Killings are already too strong for a weak self and nothing restrains them, Wealth feeds the thing that is attacking him, and the manuals have a phrase for the result with no softness in it: 因妻致祸, calamity arrived at through the wife.

Resource is what the wife star attacks. Wealth controls Resource, as every element controls another, and for marriage reading this is the relation with the longest shadow, because a man's Resource is his mother, his schooling, his protection, everything that fed him before he fed himself. A man whose chart leans on its Resource — a weak self under strong Authority, kept alive by the element that converts pressure into support — and whose Wealth then stands out and breaks that Resource has a wife star that is the disease of his chart. The tradition's name for the condition is 贪财坏印 (tān cái huài yìn), greed for Wealth ruining the Seal, which is the old name for Resource. The texts themselves say only that Wealth harms the mother. The plainest domestic meaning of that is this book's gloss and every culture's experience: the man standing between his wife and his mother. And the reverse case is equally real. A man smothered in Resource — over-sheltered, over-taught, supplied past the point of ever needing to act — is the one for whom Wealth is medicine. The wife is what gets him out of his mother's house.

Ren Tieqiao set the whole doctrine out as two triads, and they can be put side by side.

| The chart's condition | What the Wealth does | His verdict |
|---|---|---|
| The self strong, the Killings shallow | nourishes the Killings | the wife worthy and fair, or fortune reached through her |
| The Officer light, the Hurting Officer heavy | converts the Hurting Officer | the same |
| Resource piled layer on layer | stands in vigor against it | the same |
| The Killings heavy, the self light | joins the Killings' party | the wife unworthy, or harm met through her |
| Officers many, the chart relying on Resource | ruins the Resource | the same |
| The Hurting Officer wearing the Seal | forms a frame and breaks it | the same |

Read down the middle column. In the first three rows the Wealth is doing something the chart needed done; in the last three it is undoing something the chart depended on. Nothing else distinguishes them. The "worthy" wife and the "unworthy" wife of the right-hand column are not two kinds of woman. They are the same star met by two kinds of chart, and Ren ends the list by saying so: 皆以四柱情势、日主喜忌而论 — all of it is to be judged by the disposition of the four pillars and by what the Day Master welcomes and shuns.

### The strength of a wife

So the first question, whether a man has the strength of a wife, is the question of the last chapter put to a man: is the Wealth on the side of what the chart needs, can the self carry it, and is the seat at peace. The manuals' positive signs are all versions of that. 财命有气，妻妾和顺，是得妻力: Wealth and self both with vigor. 日下月下坐财官，主妻多内助，更得妻财: Wealth or Officer seated beneath the day or the month, much help from within and gain by her besides. 财星得位，因妻致富成家: the Wealth star in its proper position, a household and a fortune made through the wife. And two lines in the idiom of the oldest rhapsodies, where the horse is a name for Wealth and the stipend a name for the Officer: 马入妻宫，必得能家之妇 — the horse entering the wife's palace, a woman who can run a house is surely won; 禄入妻宫，食妻之禄 — the stipend entering the wife's palace, one eats of the wife's stipend.

Ren Tieqiao adds four cases on combination that complete the picture, and they are so compact that they are best given whole:

> 日主坐财，财为喜用者，必得妻财。日主喜财，财合闲神而化财者，必得妻力。日主喜财，财合闲神而化忌神者，主妻有外情。日主忌财，财合闲神而化财者，主琴瑟不和。
>
> Where the Day Master sits on Wealth and Wealth is what it welcomes and uses, there will surely be gain through the wife. Where the Day Master welcomes Wealth, and the Wealth combines with an idle god and transforms into Wealth, there will surely be the strength of a wife. Where the Day Master welcomes Wealth, and the Wealth combines with an idle god and transforms into what the chart shuns, the wife's heart is elsewhere. Where the Day Master shuns Wealth, and the Wealth combines with an idle god and transforms into more Wealth, the zither and the lute are out of tune.

The zither and the lute are the tradition's oldest image of a married couple; it comes from the Odes, where a loving union of wife and children is like the playing of the two together. The third case is the one the manuals elsewhere tell as scandal, and Ren's version shows what the scandal was measuring. A wife star that a man needs, drawn by combination into the service of something that harms him, is a marriage whose energy is being spent against him. Whether that takes the form of an affair is the least of what the chart can say and the least reliable.

### What she is like

The honest answer is that a man's chart describes his wife much less than it describes his marriage, and the best classical authors keep to that. What can be read is of three kinds.

The first is the star's condition, which describes how the marriage weighs. A wife star in vigor and welcome is a wife who is, in his life, a capable presence. A wife star declining, in its Bathing, entombed or extinct — 财衰败墓绝 — is in the manual's words a wife ailing or unhelpful, or, as it adds with period candor, a much older woman married after another husband. A wife star clear in Ren Tieqiao's sense is the worthy mistress of the house; a turbid one is the lioness.

The second is the palace, which describes the ground on which she is met. Part Two gives the ten gods in the spouse palace one by one. Here it is enough to recall the rule of Chapter Three, that what the palace holds is what the self expects of intimacy and brings into the room, and that a man seated on his Resource expects to be looked after, a man seated on his Output expects to give and to perform, a man seated on Authority expects to be held to a standard at home, and a man seated on his own kind expects a companion and gets, along with her, a contest.

The third is direct and indirect. A chart whose only Wealth is the direct star tends to a marriage that is legible to the world: steady, earned, conventional in form. A chart whose only Wealth is the indirect star tends to one with more voltage and fewer rails, as Chapter Four put it, and often to a wife of wider horizons than his own. A chart with both, and both on the Stems, has the divided attention of Shen Xiaozhan's fourth sentence. And a chart whose direct star is hidden while the indirect is shown is the one the rhapsody means by 出现偏财，少爱正妻偏爱妾 — the man whose visible reaching and whose real bond are not in the same place.

One line in the manuals deserves a paragraph to itself because a modern reader will stumble on it. 财官并美，为人怕妻: where Wealth and Officer are both fine, the man is one who fears his wife. It is said without contempt, as a plain correlate of a well-made chart. A strong, clean Wealth generating a strong, clean Officer is a marriage that has authority in it — a wife whose judgment has consequences in his life. The tradition, for all its household law, counted that among the marks of fortune.

### Early and late

The rules for when a man marries belong to Chapter Ten, but the natal chart already tilts the answer, and the old lines say which way.

For early marriage there is one rule, and it is about the first Luck Pillars: 欲识男儿早娶，定是运合财乡 — to know the boy who marries early, it is surely that his luck meets the country of Wealth. If the decades of his late teens and twenties bring his Wealth star, or combine with it, or complete a frame of it, the marriage comes then. A quiet or combined palace helps; so does a star that is declared and unrobbed. 妻星明朗，乔木相求: where the wife star is bright and clear, the tall tree is sought — the good match comes looking.

For late marriage there are several, and they are the same conditions that threaten a marriage's peace, which is the tradition's way of saying that delay is the mild form of difficulty. 比劫重婚必迟: where Companions are heavy, marriage must be late. 财绝官囚，妻迟子晚: the Wealth extinct and the Officer imprisoned, wife late and children later. 妻宫有克，少年无早娶之人; 日时冲者，妻子招迟 — the struck palace of Chapter Three. A chart with no Wealth in it waits for a decade that brings some. A chart whose Wealth is in storage waits for the year that opens the vault.

A reader should take the lesson that is already in the manuals' own lines. Almost every sign that the old books read as a wife "overcome" is also a sign of a wife late; the same texts give both readings, as the lines just quoted do for the struck palace and for the crowd of Companions. One of the compendium's rules about wives who will not last even carries an escape clause — 如克过头，妻再醮，不在此限: if the overcoming has already been gone through, or the wife is one who has been married before, the rule does not apply. Its authors, then, thought of what they were measuring as something that could be spent. This book goes a step further than they did, and the step is its own: it reads such a difficulty as one of timing and of weight, and not as a debt owed to fate in the form of a dead spouse.

### What "overcoming the wife" measures

The phrase 克妻 has done more damage than any other two characters in this art, and since this book must use the texts in which it appears, it should say once, plainly, what the phrase covers. Collect every sign the manuals attach it to and they fall into five groups.

There are the signs that the star is robbed: Companions heavy, Blades many, Wealth thin. There are the signs that the star is too heavy: Wealth abundant, self weak, no Companions. There are the signs that the star is an enemy of the structure: Wealth breaking a needed Resource, Wealth feeding ungoverned Killings. There are the signs that the star is unavailable: entombed, extinct, combined away, absent. And there are the signs that the palace is disturbed: the seat clashed or punished, the self seated on its own kind, the palace in the Void.

Not one of the five is a statement about a woman. Each is a statement about a relation in a man's own chart — that he competes with his marriage, or is crushed by it, or is damaged by it, or cannot reach it, or cannot keep its ground still. The old world, which had only one name for a marriage that failed and only one acceptable way for it to end, wrote down all five as the death of a wife. Chen Su'an's objection to the stars of ill omen applies to the phrase itself: in any month, sons are born by the thousand with strong Companions and thin Wealth, and their wives do not die. What those men have is a chart in which the bond of marriage is under one specific kind of strain. The reading names the strain. And then, by the logic of disease and medicine, it asks what answers it.

### The charts

His chart has now been read as a man's chart in pieces across four chapters, and the pieces can be assembled against the four questions.

Does he have the strength of a wife? By Shen Xiaozhan's test, yes: the wife star belongs to the structure the chart is built on, and the palace beneath him holds the god that keeps him standing. By the weighing, the strength is more than he can use: Wealth in command of the season and the self Imprisoned. Run Ren Tieqiao's cases. Wealth heavy, the self weak, and no Companions is his second case of an injured marriage — and the chart fails the third condition, three times over. The Rivals in the seat, in the Hour and in the Year's vault are the difference between a man crushed by his marriage and a man burdened by it. Here the two aphorisms about Companions meet in one chart and can be watched contradicting each other. 身坐比肩成比局: he is seated on his own kind, and with the Dragon his seat forms the half of a Water frame that every Monkey year or Monkey decade completes. By the old line, and by Zhang Nan's verse upon it, that is the bridegroom several times over. By Ren's correction, and by the first book's whole diagnosis, the completed frame is the best thing that ever happens to this chart — the first book said of the decade that brought it that the season was, for once, overruled. The verse has seen something true: the mass of Water does stand against the Fire that is the wife star. It has drawn the wrong conclusion, because it never asked which of the two the chart was short of.

And the bridge is there. Between his Water and his Fire stands Wood: the Eating God on the Month Stem directly over the wife star, the Hurting Officer in the Hour. The Companions of this chart do not fall on the Wealth. They pour into the Output, and the Output feeds the Wealth. It is an expensive arrangement for a weak Day Master — the first book counted the drain — but as a marriage signature it is the benign one: what this man gives to his wife star passes through his work.

What is she like, by this chart? The star's condition says a capable presence, more than capable: Yin Fire at its own Office, in season, fed. The steady bond is the hidden one, under the working life, and the declared one is the Windfall on the horizon — the rhapsody's line about the indirect star that shows. The palace says he expects a companion in his own element and will find, with her, a contest about what is whose; the first book said as much when it first counted his gods. And one of Ren's remarks fits the hidden star with some precision: 财星深伏，宜冲动而引助, a Wealth star lying deep wants a clash to stir it and draw it out. The clash between his seat and his month, which Chapter Three read as the disturbance of the palace, is also the thing that rouses the Direct Wealth inside the Horse. The same collision that keeps the room from being still is what brings the wife star into play. That is not a contradiction either. It is what the first book meant by attraction and collision in one place.

Early or late? The rule is 运合财乡, and his Luck Pillars answer it with unusual clarity. The second decade, from twelve to twenty-two, brings Yang Fire on its Stem: the Windfall, the visible star, the bright and distant one. The third, from twenty-two to thirty-two, is 丁酉 — Yin Fire itself on the Stem, the hidden Direct Wealth brought to the surface at last and combining with the Day Master the moment it appears. The first book called it the marriage signature of the sequence. By the rhapsody it is the textbook case: the luck, quite literally, combines with the Wealth.

Does it hold? Everything in the first three answers says there is a marriage here and a substantial one. The fourth question is the palace's, and the palace's answer has not changed since Chapter Three: a seat clashed at full strength by the Branch that houses the star. Of the five things that "overcoming the wife" can measure, his chart shows two — the star too heavy, and the ground unquiet. It does not show the star robbed, since the Companions are needed; nor the star an enemy of the structure, since the structure is built on it; nor the star unavailable. The first strain has its medicine in Metal and Water. The second waits, as the last chapter said, for something that stills a clash.

Before leaving the man's reading, try an experiment that shows how much of it depends on the sex of the person and how much does not. Take the woman's eight characters and suppose for one paragraph that they belonged to a man. His wife stars would be Metal. The direct one, Yin Metal, is nowhere in the chart. The indirect one, Yang Metal, is everywhere: on the Year Stem, on the Hour Stem, and at the head of both Monkeys, four times in all, in one form, so that Shen Xiaozhan's fourth sentence does not apply but his weighing does. Metal in a Rabbit month is Imprisoned, and there are four of it against a Yang Fire that is weak and has no companion on any Stem. That is 财多身弱 to the letter — the poor man in the rich house, the first book's own diagnosis of the man of this canon, arrived at from the other side of the five phases. Ren Tieqiao's second case of an injured marriage, Wealth heavy, the self weak, and no Companions, would be read against him in full. Then the palace: 丙申 is one of the twelve pillars of Chapter Three's fourth row, a man seated on his own wife star, with the Meng Guang gloss and its condition — unclashed — and the Tiger at his Hour clashes it. A matchmaker of the better kind would have read him a marriage larger than himself, in a room that could not keep still, and would have told him to grow before he married.

None of that is in her chart, because she is not a man. For her the four Metals are her Wealth — her competence, her stewardship, and, as Chapter Eleven will show, the house of the family she marries into — and the Water they feed is her husband. Not a syllable of the marriage reading carries over. What carries over without change is the palace: the Monkey beneath the day, three-chambered and struck from the Hour, would be exactly the same room for either. The star follows the sex of the person. The palace does not. That is why this book has kept the tradition's two stars and has also kept insisting that they are the smaller half of the matter — and it is time to read her chart by the star that is actually hers.

---

## Chapter Seven — The Woman's Chart

推妇人之命，与男命大不同, the old manual begins: to read the fate of a woman is a very different thing from reading the fate of a man. Every later author repeats the sentence, and the long chapters that follow it — on the eight methods and the eight formats, on the pure and the turbid, on the stars that make a wife and the stars that unmake one — are the most elaborate thing the classical literature built on the subject of marriage. They are also where it is at its worst. A reader who wants to use this material must first see exactly what the difference between the two readings consists of, because most of it is not a difference in method at all.

There are four differences, and only the first is technical.

The first is the star. A woman's spouse star is the element that controls her Day Master, where a man's is the element his Day Master controls. Chapter One derived both from the same five marriages.

The second is that the manuals read a second star alongside the first, as they never do for a man. 三命通会 opens its treatise by asking what profits a woman, and answering:

> 利在夫星。夫利，其妇必利；夫困，其妇必困。妇人从夫，先观夫星以定出身之贵贱，再看子星以察晚年之荣辱。
>
> Her profit lies in the husband star. Where the husband prospers, the wife must prosper; where the husband is straitened, the wife must be straitened. A woman follows her husband: look first at the husband star to settle whether her station will be high or low, and then at the child star to see whether her late years will be honored or shamed.

Husband star and child star — 夫子二星, the pair Zhang Nan told the man's family to look for. In a world where a woman's standing was her husband's and her old age was her sons', those two stars were her biography.

The third is a different ideal of strength. Of a man the texts ask that he be strong enough to carry what his chart lays on him. Of a woman they ask something else, and Chen Su'an states it as the first principle of the whole reading: 喜柔不喜刚，喜静不喜动 — softness is welcome and hardness is not; stillness is welcome and movement is not. The older manual is blunter: 女命只要身弱 — a woman's chart need only be weak in the self, for then she is gentle, serves her parents-in-law, and is a help to her husband; and 身强欺夫, a strong self bullies the husband.

The fourth is the stars of ill omen, which swarm over the women's chapters as they swarm nowhere else: Peach Blossom and the Traveling Horse, the Lone Phoenix, the Widow's Lodge, the Eight Defeats, and behind them a vocabulary of contempt that the men's chapters never needed.

Now look at what is left when the second, third and fourth are taken for what they are. The second is not a method; it is a statement about what a woman's life was allowed to contain. The third is the household law of Chapter One written as a preference about elements. The fourth is what Chapter Two has already disposed of, and 滴天髓 disposes of it again in a verse:

> 论夫论子要安详，气静平和妇道章，三奇二德虚好语，咸池驿马半推详。
>
> In judging husband and child, what is wanted is serenity. Where the breath is still and level, the way of a wife shines forth. The Three Wonders and the Two Virtues are empty pretty words; of Peach Blossom and the Traveling Horse, believe the half.

The old note under it is curter than the verse: the Virtues and the Wonders need not be discussed at all, and as for the Peach Blossom and the Horse, even where they seem to prove true, 总之于理不长 — in the end there is not much reason in them.

What remains is the first difference and nothing else. A woman's chart is read with the same grammar as a man's — Chen Su'an says so directly: wealth and poverty, long life and short, are worked out from the same structures — and with one relation pointed the other way. This chapter reads the tradition's long treatise on women in that spirit. It keeps the technique, which is fine-grained and often acute. It translates the furniture. And where the furniture cannot be translated, it says so.

### The neighbours of the husband star

As for the man in the last chapter, each of the other four families of gods stands in a fixed relation to the star, and each has two faces.

Wealth is the husband star's source. The element a woman controls is the element that generates what controls her, so the Wealth in her chart feeds her Officer — the second half of the old manual's first line: 财旺生官，则夫纳福, where Wealth is vigorous and generates the Officer, the husband receives blessing. A verse that 三命通会 quotes as scripture makes it a formula: 财官印绶三般物，女命逢之必旺夫 — Wealth, Officer and Seal, three things; a woman's chart that meets them surely prospers her husband. The meaning in a life is not obscure. A woman's Wealth is what she manages and what she is competent in, and the tradition observed that this competence is what a marriage runs on. Chen Su'an adds the caution that makes it a judgment and not a slogan: 财以资夫，宜轻宜旺有别 — Wealth supplies the husband, but whether it should be light or abundant differs from chart to chart; a weak self cannot carry much of it, and Wealth that swamps a needed Resource is as harmful here as in a man's chart.

Output is the husband star's adversary — and it is also the child star. This is the one structural fact about a woman's chart that has no counterpart in a man's, and nearly everything distinctive in the old treatise follows from it. The element she generates is the element that controls what controls her. The Hurting Officer got its name from this: it is the god that wounds the Officer. 伤官见官，为祸百端. 女犯伤官，须再嫁. 伤官叠遇，克夫星而再嫁之人 — the Hurting Officer met in layers, a woman who overcomes her husband star and marries again. And yet the same god is her child: 女命取伤官是子，食神是女, the Hurting Officer her son and the Eating God her daughter. The manuals knew perfectly well what they had on their hands. 伤官主人聪明，美貌秀气, the old manual says in the middle of its warnings: the Hurting Officer makes a person intelligent, and beautiful, and of a fine air. In a woman's chart, her creative power, her speech, her children and everything she brings into the world from herself stand, by the bare cycle, against the form that marriage places on her. Half of the eight formats exist to describe how that contest comes out: the husband flourishing and the children hurt, the children flourishing and the husband hurt, both hurt, both whole.

The contest has a peacemaker, and it is Wealth. Output generates Wealth, and Wealth generates the Officer. With Wealth standing between them the adversary becomes the supplier at one remove, and child and husband are one stream. 三命通会 says it at the outset — there are women who prosper their husbands, 以食生财，财生官故耳, simply because the Eating God generates Wealth and Wealth generates the Officer — and the old verse on the Hurting Officer in a woman's chart, quoted in Chapter Five, says what the remedy is: let it carry Wealth, let it carry Resource, and the blessing is firm.

Resource is the husband star's child, and that is not the comfort it sounds. The Officer generates Resource, which means Resource draws on the Officer to live; and Resource controls Output, which means it suppresses the child star. One Resource beside a weak Day Master is the saving of a chart pressed by its Authority — it converts the pressure into support. Resource in layers is the condition the manuals fear almost as much as the Hurting Officer: 印绶重逢，不死别即生离之妇, a woman parted from her husband if not by death then in life. The mechanics are exact: in the format called "husband hurt and children overcome," the compendium explains that the heaped Resource 盗夫之气, steals the husband's breath, and bears down on the children besides. A reader who wants the human meaning should think of what Resource is: the mother's house, one's own sufficiency, the inner life that asks for nothing from outside. A woman's chart with a great deal of it is the chart of someone who is already supplied — and the bond of marriage, which lives on being needed for something, is drained by exactly that.

Companions are the husband star's rivals. What shares her element is controlled by the same Officer and combines with the same Stem. Chen Su'an's line is the fair one: 比劫帮身，毕竟争官分食 — Companions help the self, and in the end they contend for the Officer and divide the food. Where two Stems of her own kind both reach for a single husband star, the compendium has a format for it, with a rule the reader has met: the one whose own Stem stands in vigor is the principal wife, and the other, whatever her claims, is beside the marriage and not in it.

And the star has an internal enemy, which is itself in its other form. Officer and Killings together, neither removed, is the condition every manual names first among the faults: 有官而不可见煞，有煞而不可逢官. The remedy is equally standard and equally important — one of the two must be taken out of play, by combination or by restraint. A Seven Killings governed by the Eating God is among the noblest of women's patterns, and Chen is explicit that where Officer and Killings both appear and the removing of one is done properly, there is no harm in it at all.

### Strong and weak

The preference for a weak self is the place where the old treatise most needs to be read against itself, because it does not hold to its own rule.

The lines that state the rule have been quoted. Others in the same pages say something different. The opening of 三命通会's chapter, having asked for a husband star in vigor, adds: 更得本身自旺尤佳，但旺不可太过 — and if the self too is vigorous, better still, only not to excess. The long rhapsody notes, as a plain fact, 自旺而巧于妇业，日衰而拙于女工: the woman vigorous in herself is skilled at her work, and the woman whose day is in decline is clumsy at it — though the same rhapsody has said, a line earlier, that the self in a vigorous place, however rich, sees husband and children harmed. And the format that Chapter Five took as the tradition's portrait of a good marriage has the strong self written into its definition: the husband star with vigor, 日干自旺, the Day Stem itself strong, the two evenly matched.

So the texts want three different things: a weak self, because a weak self yields; a self in central harmony; and a self strong enough to stand level with a strong husband star. They cannot all be the ideal, and the best statement the tradition made is the one that chooses: 大抵夫星要值健旺，己身须禀中和. The husband star in health and vigor; the self in central harmony. Not weakness. Matched weight.

Ren Tieqiao, who is hard on a strong self without an Officer to govern it, says of one arrangement of exactly that kind that it is the highest class of all: 财乃夫之恩星，女命身旺无官，财星得令得局者，上格也 — Wealth is the star that gives the husband life; a woman's chart with a strong self and no Officer, whose Wealth is in command of the season or formed in a frame, is of the first rank.
