(Some transcriptions print 天 for 夫, which would make Wealth Heaven's star of grace. 恩星 is the technical word for a star that generates another, and the sentence closes a run of lines that take Wealth for the husband; 夫 is the reading followed here.) A strong woman with no husband star and great competence. He did not think she was ruined. He thought she was the best case.

The reader may therefore set the preference for weakness aside, with a clear conscience and on classical authority. What a woman's chart requires for marriage is what a man's requires: that the star and the self be the same size.

### The eight methods

The classification that every manual repeats sorts women's charts under eight characters, four of praise and four of blame. Here they are with the compendium's own definitions, compressed.

| | Name | The compendium's definition |
|---|---|---|
| 纯 | pure | A single Officer, or a single Killings, with Wealth and with Resource, meeting no punishment or clash and not mixed. |
| 和 | at peace | The self soft and weak; one husband star alone; nothing in the pillars that clashes or attacks; the whole endowed with central harmony. |
| 清 | clear | One Officer or one Killings unmixed; the husband star in season; Wealth to generate the Officer and Resource to help the self; not a speck of turbid breath. |
| 贵 | noble | An Officer star with the breath of Wealth to supply it, and nothing in the four pillars that sickens it. |
| 浊 | turbid | The five phases out of their places; the self too strong; the true husband not showing and side husbands crowding in; no Wealth, Officer, Resource or Eating God in good order. |
| 滥 | profligate | Husbands many on the Stems; Wealth vigorous in the dark; Killings carried above and below. |
| 娼 | the courtesan | The self strong and the husband extinct; the Officer failing and the Eating God flourishing; or no Officer or Killings in the pillars at all; or present, and hurt to the last by the Hurting Officer. |
| 淫 | wanton | The self on its own ground and the husband stars gathering in the open and in the dark: a strong Day Stem with Officer and Killings on every side. |

Read the four of praise together and they are one chart described four times: a single husband star, unmixed, in season, supplied by Wealth, unstruck. Of the self they say little, and what the second of them says is that it should be soft and weak. Read the four of blame together and they are three conditions: the husband star in too many places; the husband star in none, with a strong self and abundant Output; and both at once. As observations about the star these are sound, and Chapters Four and Five have made every one of them without reference to anyone's virtue. A star in many forms is a divided bond. A strong self with no star is a self with nothing to engage it.

The names are another matter. The compendium does not say that a chart of the seventh kind has a difficult relation to the form of marriage. It says 此必娼妓之命，否则为师尼婢妾 — this must be the fate of a courtesan or a prostitute, and if not that, of a nun, a maidservant or a concubine. The list tells on itself. Courtesan, nun, maid, concubine: these are simply the four places that the old society kept for a woman who was not a principal wife. The chart showed a woman outside the one honored form of the bond, and the manual, knowing of no honorable way to stand outside it, read off the dishonorable ones. A reader today, looking at the same eight characters, sees what the technique actually found: a strong woman whose life is not organized around a husband. She may be unmarried, or married late, or married on terms of her own, or the head of her house. None of those is a vice, and the chart never said it was.

The tradition's own best authors refused the names in so many words. Chen Su'an: whether a woman is upright or not, gentle or harsh, is to be judged from the Day Master and the structure by the five phases alone — do not be bound by the old books' invented stars — and he ends his rhapsody on women with a warning to the diviner himself: 造诸煞以推评，贞妇恐遭轻诋. Manufacture evil stars to judge by, and a chaste wife is liable to be lightly slandered; those who enjoy talking of what is doubtful in other people's households, he adds, are mostly called to account for it by the spirits. Ren Tieqiao, after his own long list of the conditions under which a chart tends that way, pulls up short: 凡观女命，关系非小，不可轻断淫邪 — whenever one reads a woman's chart, what hangs on it is no small matter; it must never be lightly judged for vice. And then he says something the manuals had not said: that such things are not to be put down to the chart in every case — that they come as often from the fortunes of a house, from a husband who is worthless, from a mother or a mother-in-law who is no good. His own remedy, it must be added, is the old world's, a stricter watch on the women's quarters. But the admission is made, and it is not a small one.

### The eight formats

The second classification is more useful, because seven of its eight headings describe outcomes and not characters, and one of them is the portrait already quoted.

| | Format | What the compendium means by it |
|---|---|---|
| 旺夫克子 | husband prospers, children hurt | Read at the Hour, where both stars come to rest: the husband star arrives there in vigor, the child star in decline. |
| 旺子伤夫 | children prosper, husband hurt | The husband star out of position and out of season, struck in the pillars and unsupported at the Hour, while the child she bears arrives at the Hour at its Birth, Office or Peak. |
| 伤夫克子 | husband and children both hurt | The husband star out of position and season and clashed, with Resource in layers stealing its breath and bearing down on the child star as well. |
| 安静守分 | quiet, keeping to her part | The husband star with vigor, the Day Stem itself strong, the two evenly matched, no punishment or clash, Wealth and Eating God each in place. |
| 福寿两备 | blessing and long life together | The self seated in vigor and connected to the month, Stems and Branches supporting one another, with Wealth, Officer and Resource each in its position. |
| 正偏自处 | principal or secondary by her own standing | Husband and wife combine, and a Companion contends: one husband star with two of her kind reaching for it. Where her own Stem is in vigor and the other's in decline, she is the principal. |
| 招嫁不定 | the marriage unsettled | A husband star in the Month, revealed and combining with her, but without vigor, while another, or a Killings, stands at the Hour in strength. If the husband star is weak or restrained, she marries late, or irregularly, or the husband is ineffectual, or her heart is elsewhere. |
| 横夭少年 | cut off young | A weak self under heavy Killings, with punishment and clash besides. The compendium itself notes that this one is not peculiar to women. |

Three things in this table are worth taking away.

The first is the use of the Hour. The compendium calls it 归宿之地, the place where things come home to rest, and reads both stars by the life stage at which they arrive there. Where the husband star reaches the Hour at its Office or its Peak and the child star reaches it at its Death or Extinction, husband prospers and children are hurt; reverse the stages and reverse the verdict. It is a neat instrument, and it works for either sex.

The second is that the first three formats describe a single balance: the husband star against the child star, Authority against Output. That balance is the real content of a woman's chart, and it does not need the old world to mean something. A life in which what a woman makes and a life in which what holds her are in proportion is the fourth format. A life in which one has eaten the other is one of the first three.

The third is the seventh format's list of what a weak or restrained husband star may mean, because it is the most honest sentence in the treatise. Late marriage, or irregular marriage, or a husband who is no use, or an attachment elsewhere: four different lives, and the chart, by the compendium's own admission, cannot say which. It can say only that the bond is under-powered.

### The husband by function

Chapter Four gave the old note to 滴天髓 on the husband taken by function. Ren Tieqiao's commentary works it out into a full table, and since this is the tradition's last word on the subject it should be set down whole.

| The chart | The husband is |
|---|---|
| The Officer too strong, and no Companions | Resource |
| The Officer too strong, with Companions and no Resource | Output |
| The Officer too weak, with a Hurting Officer present | Wealth |
| The Officer too weak, no Wealth, Companions strong | Output |
| Companions filling the chart, no Resource and no Officer | Output |
| Resource filling the chart, no Officer and no Output | Wealth |
| The Hurting Officer strong and the Day Master weak | Resource |
| The Day Master strong and Output abundant | Wealth |
| The Officer light and Resource heavy | Wealth |

Every line is the same act of reasoning. Find what the chart is suffering from; the husband is what answers it. 凡女命之夫星，即是用神，女命之子星，即是喜神: the husband star of a woman's chart simply is the Useful God, and the child star simply is the Auspicious God. It is the counterpart of the seasonal handbook's rule for a man, in which it is the child that is the Useful God.

His list of the conditions under which a husband star is harmed repays the same kind of reading as his list for men in the last chapter. There are eight, and they need not be copied out; what matters is what they share. In most of them the absence of Wealth is written into the condition itself: 无财星, no Wealth star. A weak Officer beside strong Companions, with no Wealth. A weak Officer under heavy Resource, with no Wealth. Strong Companions and strong Resource with no Officer at all — and no Wealth. In a woman's chart, almost every injury to the husband star is an injury that Wealth would have prevented: it feeds the Officer, it converts the Hurting Officer, and it holds the Resource in check. That is why Ren calls it the star that gives the husband life.

### Early, late, and unsettled

For the timing of a woman's marriage the rhapsodies give a rule in the same breath as the man's: 要知女命难婚，运入背夫之位 — to know the woman for whom marriage comes hard, her luck has entered the place that turns its back on the husband. Decades that carry her husband star to its Death or Extinction, or that bring the god that strikes it with nothing to convert the blow, are decades in which the bond does not form or does not hold; decades that bring the star, or bring what feeds it, or open the vault where it is stored, are the ones in which it does. Two darker lines belong to the same rule. 四柱有官鬼入墓，使夫星久入黄泉: where the Officer in the four pillars has entered its tomb, the husband star is long gone to the Yellow Springs. 岁运临夭绝之宫，俾鸳配分飞异路: where year and luck arrive at the palace of its extinction, the paired birds fly apart on different roads. Take them as Chapter Six took the lines about wives. They measure the star's unavailability, and a star that is stored or extinct in one decade is neither in the next.

### The charts

Her chart, read as the old treatise would read it, comes first, and the reader should watch how many times it is condemned before the reading has properly begun.

Her husband star is the Seven Killings, and only that. 七杀是偏夫: the Killings is the irregular husband. It stands on no Stem, and the rhapsody on women's charts has a line for a husband found in the Branches of the day: 偏夫者，夫旺于日时之上. Her Hurting Officer stands on a Stem. 女犯伤官，须再嫁. Her Wealth is four times in the chart. 财多而淫，故女人要财薄: much Wealth is wantonness, so a woman's Wealth should be thin. Her palace is clashed by her Hour. 时日对冲，伤妻克子, and for a woman that reads husband and children. And the compendium's instrument of the Hour, applied to her: the husband star arrives there at its Sickness and the child star at its Birth, which is the letter of the format called children prosper, husband hurt.

Five verdicts, and they were reached in the time it takes to count the Stems. Now read the chart.

The star, by the first definition, is the Killings alone, and Chapter Four has already given Zhang Nan's ruling, which disposes of the first verdict: where there is no Direct Officer, the Seven Killings is the husband proper. The manuals' real fear was mixing, and nothing in her chart is mixed. Of the eight methods she comes nearest the first, 纯, which the compendium itself defines as a single Officer or a single Killings, with Wealth and with Resource, meeting no punishment or clash and not mixed. She has the single Killings, the Wealth and the Resource. She fails the condition about the clash, and the reading will come to that. Ren Tieqiao's table of husbands by function does not single her out: her husband star is neither too strong nor so faint that the Wealth must stand in for it, and so by his own general rule the star is the husband and the question is whether it is the Useful God. Chapter Five answered that in the handbook's terms. For a Yang Fire of the second month the one god to use is Yang Water, and she has it twice. (The handbook's own formula for a woman — take its wife for the husband and its Useful God for the child — would give her a husband of Metal and a child of Water, as Chapter Four said. This book follows Ren.)

So the corrections. For the Hurting Officer, the logic of the god itself: it wounds the Officer, and she has none to wound; what it meets in her chart is the Killings, and the old lines welcome that meeting — 偏官有制化为权, the Killings under restraint turns into authority. More than that, the Hurting Officer in her chart does not fall on the Killings at all. It stands over Wealth, and Wealth stands over the Killings. 伤官尤喜见财星, the Hurting Officer is glad above all to see the Wealth star; and the compendium's reason why some women prosper their husbands was quoted at the outset, 以食生财，财生官故耳 — because the Output feeds the Wealth and the Wealth feeds the Officer. Hers is that chain, in its indirect forms: the Hurting Officer feeding the Windfall feeding the Killings. For the Wealth, Ren Tieqiao's line from the same page, 财乃夫之恩星: Wealth is the star that gives the husband life. The prudish sentence about a woman's Wealth being thin measured, as this book has said, a household's convenience and not a person's fortune; what four Metals do in this chart is nourish a husband star that is hidden and young.

Now the conditions in Ren's list that do touch her, since a reading that only defends is no better than one that only accuses. There are two, and both are written across the letter of her chart. The eighth of his cases of a husband harmed is this: 食神多，官星微，有印绶，遇财星，必克夫 — many Eating Gods, a faint Officer, Resource present, and Wealth met: the husband is surely overcome. She has Yang Earth in three of her four Branches, a husband star that never shows, the Seal in command, and four Metals. And among the conditions he lists as tending toward disorder, before he forbids the inference, is 日主弱，伤食重，印绶轻 — a weak self, heavy Output, light Resource. The mechanism of both is the same, and Chapter Five found it: the Wealth that should feed her husband star also breaks the Seal that should restrain her Output, and an Output unrestrained falls on a faint husband star. That is the hazard of her chart, and it is the only one. Its answer is also in Chapter Five. Four Earths standing beside four Metals give to the Metal before they strike the Water; Ren's own list elsewhere makes Wealth the thing that turns the Hurting Officer, and the handbook wants Metal declared beside the Water for exactly this reason. Her Wealth is the mediator between her Output and her husband star, and her husband star is the mediator between her Wealth and her Seal. Both mediations run through what she has most of. What the two conditions describe, when the moral furniture is taken off them, is a self that spends more than it is given, and a husband star that would be at the mercy of that spending if the Wealth between them were ever cut. The decades and years that strike her Metal, or tie it up, are the ones that release the hazard. Chapter Ten will look for them.

The compendium's instrument of the Hour gives the fifth verdict, and it should be weighed as the compendium weighs it. Yang Water at the Tiger is at its Sickness; Yang Earth, which keeps Fire's calendar, is at its Birth there. By the stages alone that is children prosper, husband hurt. But the format's full definition asks for a husband star out of position and out of season, struck in the pillars and unsupported at the Hour, and hers is in position, in the palace itself, rooted twice at its own Birth. What the instrument has found is narrower and true: the Hour — her children, her private world, her later years — is the one domain of her life in which her own Fire is born and her husband star is weakest. The marriage has least say there.

What kind of bond is it, with the Killings for a husband? Not the one the word Officer describes. The Officer is the magistrate: form, standing, the line one is held to, with one's consent. The Killings is the reference's Merciless, the force that dominates without negotiation and either crushes or forges; and of a Killings under restraint the tradition says what was quoted above, that it turns into authority. A woman whose husband star is the governed Killings does not look to a partner for rank or for rule. She looks to him as pressure — the thing that will not let her rest in what she has already made — and her chart is built to convert that pressure into standing, through the Eating God beneath the star and the Wealth above it. The partner as the force that forges, not the partner as the law. That is this book's reading of what the relation implies; the sources say only that the husband is to be read from that god.

Where does he stand? In her palace, as the second tenant, and again in the Branch of her Year: in the room she lives in and in the ground she came from. On the Monkey, where Yang Water has its Birth, twice. At Rest in his season. Fed from above by her Wealth and governed from below by her Provider. And the compendium's instrument, applied to him at the Hour, returns Sickness, not Death or the Tomb: weak where her children are, strong where she lives. The star is young, rooted, kept, and never shown. What it waits for is a Yang Water in the open.

The compendium has one more technique, and it is worth borrowing once. In its specimen readings it does not stop at finding the husband star; it reads the husband's own gods from the wife's chart — his Officer, his Wealth, his Eating God — taking her husband star as if it were a Day Master. Take Yang Water as the husband and look at her chart through him. His Direct Officer is Yin Earth: it stands on her Month Stem, immediately beside her — what holds him to a line is what she says. His Resource is Metal: it is at the head of her palace and on two of her Stems — what feeds him is what she carries. His Eating God is Yang Wood: it lies in the Tiger of her Hour — what he makes is in her private world. And his wife star is Fire, which is she herself. The husband her eight characters describe is a man of Yang Water fed by her Wealth, governed by her tongue, whose talent lives where her children are, and whose wife is herself. The reader who has read the first book will have noticed that the man of this canon is a Yang Water with no Metal of his own and too much Fire, and will know what to make of that.

One paragraph, last, on the experiment of the previous chapter turned round. Suppose the man's eight characters were a woman's. Her husband stars would be Earth, and both are hidden: the Killings as the head of the Dragon at the Year, the Officer as the lesser Stem inside the Horse at the Month. Wealth in command of the season feeds them. The Eating God on the Month Stem governs them. And the self is weak. An old-fashioned reader of women's charts would have been pleased with nearly all of it: 女命只要身弱, vigorous Wealth generating the Officer, the Killings under restraint. The same eight characters that the first book diagnosed as a man overdrawn — too much asked, too little supplied — would have been commended in a woman for exactly that. Nothing shows more plainly what the old preference for the weak self was. It did not find such charts fortunate for the person. It found them convenient for the marriage. And the palace, once again, would not have changed by a single stroke: the Rat beneath the day, clashed by the month, for either.

Both charts have now been read alone, his as a man's and hers as a woman's. He is a weak self under a wife star too heavy for him, on a seat that helps him and cannot be still. She is a weak self with a husband star kept in her own room, fed and governed and never shown, on a seat struck by her own beginnings. It is time to put them in one room.

---

## Chapter Eight — Two Charts

Seven chapters in, a book about couples has not yet compared one. That was deliberate, and it was the classical order. But the reader was promised a second chart, and here it is admitted — with a warning about how little the tradition says of what to do with it.

There are three ways to set two charts side by side, and they are of very unequal authority.

The first is to read each alone and ask whether it is the chart of someone who can carry a marriage. This is what Zhang Nan's two tests and Chen Su'an's single sentence prescribe; it rests on the whole classical method; and the last five chapters have done it.

The second is to ask what each chart does to the balance of the other: whether what one is made of is what the other lacks, or what the other already has too much of. For this there is one explicit classical rule, Zhang Nan's; one explicit early-modern method, Yuan Shushan's; and beneath both, the logic of disease and medicine, which was never confined to a single chart. This chapter is about that second comparison.

The third is to look at where the two charts touch — Stem against Stem, Branch against Branch, his Day Pillar against hers. This is what most people now mean by compatibility, and it has the thinnest classical warrant of the three. It is the next chapter's subject.

### A person is like a year

By what right is one person's chart read against another's at all? The first book taught how a pillar arriving from outside — a decade, a year — acts on a natal chart: by generation and control, by combination and clash. It did not say that another human being is such an arrival. The license for saying so is in 滴天髓, in the old note on luck and the years:

> 日主譬如吾身，局中之神譬如舟马引从，大运譬如所历之地，故重地支，未尝无天干，太岁譬如所遇之人，故重天干，未尝无地支。
>
> The Day Master is like my own body. The gods within the chart are like my boat and horse, my guides and my followers. The Luck Pillar is like the country I am passing through, and so its Branch is weighted more, though the Stem is never absent. The year is like a person I meet, and so its Stem is weighted more, though the Branch is never absent.

The year is like a person one meets. The commentator meant it as a figure for how a year acts: suddenly, as an agent with a face, more by what it declares than by the ground it stands on. Turn the figure round and it is a doctrine. A person one meets is like a year: a set of Stems and Branches that comes up beside one's own and acts on it by the same relations. The compendium had already, on occasion, read one life in another's chart. When a father is ill, it says, examine the chart of the son, and if the marks of mourning have arrived there the father will not recover — 夫妻亦准此推, and husband and wife are to be judged by the same rule. Those who share a house stand in each other's weather.

Two differences from a year must be kept in view, and they are what make the reading of couples its own subject. A year passes. A spouse stays: the eight characters beside one's own do not turn over at the next new year, so that whatever they do, they do for as long as the two share a life. And a year has no chart of its own to be acted on. A spouse has. Every relation between two charts runs in both directions, and the two directions need not agree.

This book treats the reading of one person's chart against another's by the grammar of the years as an extension of the classical method, licensed by the classical figure and practiced by every modern reader, and not as something a classical author wrote down. The reader was promised that the sentence would be there when the ground changed. This is it.

### The one rule

Zhang Nan's rule was quoted in Chapter Two: a man heavy with Companion and Rob Wealth must be matched with a woman heavy with Hurting Officer and Eating God, and the reverse. The miscellany that closes 命理约言 repeats it and says for whom it is meant: if everyone were to pick only the well-fated to marry, then 下命者, those whose charts are of the lower sort, should be paired in this way — 庶几近理, and that would come near to reason. It is a rule for the unbalanced, which is to say, for nearly everyone.

Neither text explains why these two excesses answer each other. The reasons can be recovered from Chapters Six and Seven.

A man heavy with Companions has one specific disease: his wife star is robbed. A woman heavy with Output has one specific disease: her husband star is struck. In the idiom of the marketplace each of them has a "hard fate" for a spouse, and one reading of the rule is simply that the hard should marry the hard — that a man who would wear out a delicate marriage and a woman who would do the same are safer with each other than either would be with someone unarmed. There is rough sense in that, and it is probably all the rule meant to many who used it.

But the rule also has an exact form, which appears as soon as it is worked out in elements, and no classical author worked it out. What follows is this book's derivation.

The Ten Gods are relative. His Companions are whatever shares his Day Master's element; her Output is whatever her Day Master generates. What those two crowds are to each other depends entirely on how her Day Master's element stands to his, and there are five possibilities.

Suppose his element generates hers. Then his Companions are her Resource, and her Output is his Wealth. Each chart's surplus is precisely the other's remedy: her overflowing Output, which strikes her own husband star, is the wife star his chart was being robbed of; and his crowd of Companions, which plunder his Wealth, are for her the Seal that holds a runaway Hurting Officer in check — the old verse's "let it carry Resource, and the blessing is firm." Here the rule is perfect. It is medicine in both directions at once.

Suppose his element controls hers. Then his Companions are her Authority — her husband star, in mass — and her Output is the element that controls him. Her excess governs his excess. A woman whose Hurting Officer would break an ordinary Officer has met an Officer too abundant to break, and a man whose Companions nothing could restrain has met the restraint. This is the marketplace's hard-with-hard, and in elements it holds.

Suppose her element generates his. Then his Companions and her Output are the same element: what he has too much of himself is what she pours out. The rule's two conditions turn out to be one fact, a shared abundance, and whether it is a blessing depends on whether each chart wanted that element. If both did, nothing could be better. If it is the excess of both, the pairing doubles it.

Suppose the two share an element. Then her Output is his Output, and it stands as a bridge between his Companions and his Wealth: good for him. For her, his Companions are more of her own, feeding an Output already too heavy: no help, unless her chart is a strong one that needed the outlet.

Suppose, last, that her element controls his. Then her Output is his Resource, and it feeds the Companions he already has in excess. The rule fails him outright, though his Companions, being her Wealth, give her Output somewhere to go.

So the rule is exact in two of five cases, conditional in one, half-true in one, and wrong in one. Yuan Shushan saw this at the beginning of the twentieth century and said so in a sentence: the rule 似合正理，然按五行，每多抵触 — seems to accord with right principle, and yet, checked against the five phases, runs into contradiction again and again.

### Weighing by the phases

What Yuan put in its place is the first clear statement of the method that every careful reader of two charts has used since.

> 不若以男女命之五行，斟酌损益，以决从违。如男命木盛宜金者，得女命之刚金补之，则为尽美。得土生金者亦佳。得火者，较次。得水木者，则无取矣。
>
> Better to take the five phases of the man's chart and of the woman's, weigh what is to be reduced and what supplied, and so decide whether to go forward or decline. Suppose a man's chart in which Wood is in excess and Metal is wanted: if the woman's chart supplies it with hard Metal, that is perfection. One that has Earth, generating Metal, is also good. One that has Fire is of lesser worth. One that has Water or Wood has nothing to recommend it.

He gives the mirror case for a woman of hard Metal who welcomes Fire, and then the three characters that make it a method: 余仿此, and so for the rest.

Read his example as a scale. The best match brings the element the chart needs. The next brings the element that generates what it needs. The third brings the element that drains the excess — Fire, for too much Wood — which relieves the chart but also, in his example, threatens the very Metal it was waiting for. And the last brings the excess itself or what feeds it. In the first book's vocabulary the first of these is the Useful God. The second is a helper of the Useful God, which is often, though not always, what that book called the Auspicious God. The third is a lesser relief. For the last this book needs a word the first two texts did not use: the Inauspicious God (忌神 jì shén), the element a chart is already suffering from.

Two things make this more than a table. The first is that it must be done twice, once for each chart, and the results need not match. A woman may be the making of a man whose chart is the undoing of hers. The second is Yuan's other observation, which is the wisest thing in his chapter. Central harmony, he says, must not be looked for in the eight characters alone:

> 日主衰，而得比劫印绶之大运者，不可以不及论，当以得中和之气言也。
>
> Where the Day Master is weak but meets Luck Pillars of Companions and Resource, the chart must not be judged as falling short; it is to be spoken of as having attained the breath of central harmony.

A chart is balanced or not across its decades, and a person supplied by time is supplied. So is a person supplied by a person. The three places a remedy can be found, Chapter Five said, are the chart, the years, and a second chart; Yuan's sentence joins the second to the first, and his method joins the third.

One more scale runs beside this one, and the seasonal handbook is built on it: climate. A chart born in deep winter is cold and wet whatever its balance of strength, and a chart born at midsummer is hot and dry; the first wants Fire and the second Water before either wants anything else. Ren Tieqiao's list of the charts in which children do not come is in large part a list of climates — Fire blazing over parched Earth, Metal cold in cold Water — and what holds for children holds for a household. A partner brings a climate as well as an element. Two winter charts are cold together. A winter chart and a summer chart temper each other. To read two charts for climate is, again, this book's application; the principle is the handbook's.

### Translating a chart

To weigh one chart by another, the second must first be read in the terms of the first, and there is a law that makes this quick. Since each of the five relations is defined by its place in the cycle from the Day Master, the relation between two Day Masters fixes how every part of one chart appears to the other.

| Her element is to him | her Companions are his | her Output is his | her Wealth is his | her Authority is his | her Resource is his |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| the same as his | Companions | Output | Wealth | Authority | Resource |
| what he generates | Output | Wealth | Authority | Resource | Companions |
| what he controls | Wealth | Authority | Resource | Companions | Output |
| what controls him | Authority | Resource | Companions | Output | Wealth |
| what generates him | Resource | Companions | Output | Wealth | Authority |

The table reads the same with the sexes exchanged. It contains, among other things, a small theorem about the spouse stars. His wife star is his Wealth; her husband star is her Authority. Where his element controls hers, his Wealth is her own element and her Authority is his: each is the other's spouse star in person. That is the marriage of the fable, the configuration of the five combinations, and it is one case in five. Where her element generates his, his Wealth and her Authority are one and the same element, belonging to neither Day Master: the two share a spouse star, and every year that brings it brings it to both. In the other three cases the two stars are different elements, and each lies somewhere in the other's chart as a different god.

Five kinds of couple follow, according to what the two Day Masters are to each other, and Part Two takes all hundred pairings one at a time. In outline:

Where he controls her, she is his Wealth and he is her Authority. The bond has the shape the tradition called marriage: he is answerable for her and she gives him something to be answerable for; he gives her form and she gives him substance. If the two Stems are also of opposite polarity they are one of the five combinations, and the pull between them needs no explaining. Everything Chapters Four and Five said about the weight of a spouse star now applies to a person. To a man whose Wealth is thin she is the star arriving; to a man already crushed by Wealth she is more of it.

Where she controls him, the same bond runs the other way. She is his Authority and he is her Wealth: she holds him to a line, and he is what she manages. The household law had no honorable name for this couple, and the charts are indifferent to the household law. It serves a strong, loose man and a strong, capable woman, and it is hard on a man already pressed and on a woman who cannot carry what she already has.

Where she generates him, she is his Resource and he is her Output. By the monk's fourteen characters this is not the relation of husband and wife but of parent and child, and the reading should say so without embarrassment: she supplies and he expresses; he is where what she has goes. It serves a man short of support and a woman with more than she can use. It smothers a man already over-supplied, and it empties a woman already drained.

Where he generates her, the same, reversed: he supplies, she is what he pours himself into, and she turns what he gives into something of her own.

Where the two share an element they are Companions: equals, allies, rivals. Two weak charts of one element hold each other up. Two strong ones want the same plate.

And polarity colors each of the five, exactly as it does among the Ten Gods. 三命通会's remark, quoted in Chapter Four, is the rule: yang meeting yang is mostly without feeling; yin meeting yang is husband and wife singing in accord. Two Day Masters of opposite polarity meet as the direct gods — and, between the same element, as Rivals; between generator and generated, as the True Patron and the Underminer. Two of the same polarity meet as the indirect gods, with the edge the first book gave those names.

### What the method does not promise

Three cautions, before the pair.

The exchange is not arithmetic. A woman's chart with three Metals does not deposit three Metals in her husband's. What she brings him is what a year of Metal brings: a climate. She is, as a person, a standing instance of her own chart; to live beside her is to live in the weather of it. A man short of Metal who lives with a woman made of it lives where precision, discrimination and form are daily facts, and a chart can use what is daily present. That is all the claim amounts to, and it is not small.

Nor does a good exchange repair a chart that cannot carry a marriage, and the phrase needs a meaning. A chart with a disease that has a medicine can carry one; that is nearly every chart, and it is both of this book's. What cannot is a chart in which the spouse star is the enemy of everything the chart depends on, or the palace is struck past holding, or the self gives way under any bond at all. To such a chart a second chart is weather, welcome or unwelcome, and not a cure. An exchange supplies. It does not rebuild. The order of the questions has not changed because the second chart has arrived.

And the result is never clean. Every real pairing brings some of what is needed and some of what is not. The popular almanac that the Qianlong compilers demolished had one sentence in it worth saving, and they let it stand in their quotation: 婚姻之事，理无十全，中平亦吉 — in the matter of marriage there is, in the nature of things, no ten out of ten; the middling is also fortunate.

### The charts

Begin with the census, since everything else is read from it. The first book counted each visible and hidden Stem once, and the same count for her chart gives Wood two, Fire two, Earth four, Metal four, Water two. Set the two side by side, and translate each into the other's gods. His Yang Water controls her Yang Fire: the third row of the table for him, the fourth for her.

| | In his chart | which to her is | In her chart | which to him is |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Wood | three | Resource | two | Output |
| Fire | two | Companions | two | Wealth |
| Earth | two | Output | four | Authority |
| Metal | none | Wealth | four | Resource |
| Water | four | Authority | two | Companions |

Look at the one empty cell first. He has no Metal. She has all five phases, and the one she has most of, four times, is the one he has not at all. The sixteen characters hold the whole cycle, and the join is at Metal.

Now weigh her chart by his need. The first book's diagnosis: Useful God Metal, Auspicious God Water; the weight he cannot lift is Fire, and after it Earth and Wood. Her chart, to him, is four Metals and two Waters — his Resource and his Companions, six of her fourteen — and two of the Metals are declared, on her Year Stem and her Hour Stem, with the other two at the head of her Monkeys. 刚金, hard Metal, in Yuan Shushan's phrase: the needed element, brought hard, with the Earth that generates it lying beside it in four places. That is the first grade of his scale. Then the other half of the ledger. Her four Earths are, to him, Authority: three Killings hidden and one Officer declared, pressing on a weak Water that already has two. Her two Woods are more of the Output that drains him. And her two Fires are his Wealth, the thing he suffers from — and one of them is her Day Master, which is to say the person herself.

Yuan's scale weighs the Day Master most, then the commander of the month, then the bulk, and on the first weight she is his last grade: the sun on his horizon, in person. On the second she is a drain: her Month Command is Yin Wood, his Hurting Officer. On the third she is his first grade and his fourth at once, four Metals and four Earths. A reading that smoothed this would be lying. What she is, by the method, is the element he cannot carry; what she carries is the element he has never had. The next chapter will show that the Metal she carries also does, in his chart, exactly what the first book said Metal would do when the decades delivered it, cutting his Output as it feeds him. Here it is enough to say that the weighing of her by him comes out divided, with the person on the wrong side of the scale and her world on the right.

One qualification belongs to the person, and it is this book's, not Yuan's. He counts the phases; Chapter Five weighed her Fire. It is weak, two presences in fourteen, and her own chart spends it: the whole flow of her eight characters runs from Fire into Earth and from Earth into Metal, so that what a Yang Fire of this kind brings into a house is less heat than its Day Master promises and more of the Metal it has made of itself. That is an argument about proportion, and it does not change the grade. It says what the grade will feel like.

Weigh his chart by hers, and the picture is simpler. Her needs were two, and Chapter Five said which reckoning gives which. By the first book's method she wants Fire first and Wood after, her own element and the one that generates it; by the handbook she wants Yang Water; and what she has too much of is Metal, and after it Earth. His chart, to her, is four Waters, three Woods, two Fires, two Earths, and of Metal nothing. By the handbook he is her first grade in the Day Master itself: the Yang Water the second month's sun is told to use, declared, rooted on its Blade, with Yin Water beside it. By the strength method he is her second grade and nearly her first: three Resources and two Companions, five of his eleven, the Wood in the open on his Month and the Fire in the open on his Year. His two Earths are her Output, a small drain. And he brings none of the one element she has too much of. There is nothing in his chart that is her excess.

Two of his Waters deserve their own sentence. 癸, Yin Water, is her Direct Officer, the husband star proper, and her chart has none; his has three, on his Hour Stem and inside his Rat and his Dragon. 壬, his Day Master, is her Killings, the husband she does have. He brings her the husband star in both of its forms, the one she was born with and the one she was born without.

Is his Water welcome to her? By relation it is her Authority, and a weak self meets Authority as pressure; the old books would have weighed four Waters against a weak Fire and worried. But her chart has already told the reader what Water is for in it. An old note to 滴天髓, quoted in Chapter Five, says that where Fire is the Day Master and Wealth and Seal cross each other there must be Officer or Killings to harmonize them, and that where the chart has none the luck must bring them. Her chart has two, hidden. His Water is the mediating god of her chart, in person and in bulk: the Stem that turns her four Metals into food for her two Woods. And his Fire? It is her own element, her Companions, the first thing the strength method asks for. The Fire that is his disease is her medicine. What he has too much of is what she has too little of, and it is herself.

So by the method he is her first grade, and in the Day Master itself; and she is his first grade in what she carries and his last in what she is. Chapter Four ended on that inequality and called it one finding. It can now be said how much rests on it. Take away every Branch relation the next chapter will discuss, and this would remain: he is what she needs, and she brings what he needs.

The spouse stars deserve their sentence. His element controls hers, so by the small theorem above each is the other's spouse star in person: she is his Wealth, he is her Authority. It is the configuration of the fable, and in the same polarity, so that the stars are the indirect ones — his Windfall, her Merciless — and the Stems clash instead of combining. One consequence for time follows at once. The years of Fire, which bring him his wife star, bring her her own element: Companions, the thing her weak self wants. The years of Water, which bring her her husband star, bring him his Companions, his Auspicious God. The years that are marriage years for one of them are years of strength for the other. Chapter Ten will count them.

Zhang Nan's rule can now be answered, since Chapter Two left it hanging. He is heavy with Rob Wealth; she is heavy with Output. The rule calls for the match, and the configuration is the second of the five: his element controls hers, so that his Companions are her Authority and her Output is his Authority. The derivation above called that case exact, and it is exact on her side. Her four Earths would wound an ordinary husband star; they meet, in him, four Waters, a husband star too abundant to wound. On his side the rule misfires, and the reason is instructive. The rule assumes a man whose Companions need restraining. His do not; they are what keeps him standing. Her Output is the restraint the rule promised him, and it arrives as Authority on a self that wanted none. The rule answers her disease with his abundance, and answers a disease he does not have with an Earth he does not want.

His two tests come out the same way, and this is perhaps the most instructive result of all. Chapter Two found that neither chart passes them cleanly. The woman's side, examining him for central harmony, would not find it; the man's side, examining her for a husband star, would find it only hidden. Lay the charts together and each failure is answered from the other page. His want of balance is a want of Metal, and she carries four. Her star's want is to be declared and strong, and the Stem it is made of stands declared at the head of his Day Pillar, rooted on its Blade. These are the charts of the lower sort that the miscellany meant: each with a disease, and each holding the other's medicine, though not in equal measure. It is not the match the tradition preferred, which was of two people who needed nothing. It is the match the tradition provided for everyone else.

Last, what the two Day Masters are to each other, because it will color everything that follows. Yang Water controls Yang Fire within the same polarity. To him she is Windfall, of which the reference says:

> The resources you manage through force rather than finesse. Same polarity means the control is aggressive, direct, bold — you seize this wealth rather than cultivate it. Windfall is the unconventional gain, the speculative opportunity, the money that arrives sideways and departs just as fast.

The first book said of this man that his Wealth stood at the widest horizon, vast in scope and demanding to manage, and that he was the poor man in the rich house. It was speaking of an element. It is also a description of what it is to be married to a sun.

To her he is the Merciless:

> The force that dominates you without negotiation. Same polarity means the control is raw, aggressive, untempered — not governance but conquest. The Merciless is the power that doesn't care about your readiness, your feelings, or your consent. It crushes or it forges. There is no third option.

That is the god Chapter Seven found her chart had for its husband, kept under her Wealth and over her Provider. He does not merely bring her some of the element her husband star is made of. Seen from her Day Master, he is that star: the same Stem that lies hidden in her room, walking in at the door.

And so the caution that began this book returns in a new form. By the monk's fourteen characters these two are husband and wife: mutual restraint, the horizontal relation, in its unsoftened charge. He gives her form and she gives him something to be answerable for. There is a marriage of that shape, and the fable was written about it; it has the pull of the controlling cycle without the combination that makes the pull sweet. Its strength is that each is doing, with the other, what their own charts ask of them — he to govern a Wealth he has always had too much of, she to be forged by a pressure her chart was built to convert. Its failure, when it fails, is the one the compendium named in the sentence on yang meeting yang: two that contend. The elements say what the exchange is. They do not say with what grace it will be made. That part, as Chen Su'an said of the whole, is left to Heaven — and to the two of them.

---

## Chapter Nine — Combination and Clash

Ask anyone who has had two charts compared what was said, and the answer will be about contact. Your Day Masters combine. Your Branches clash. Her Rabbit harmonizes with his Dog; his Rooster offends hers. This is the part of the art that feels like chemistry, and it is what the word compatibility has come to mean.

It is also the part for which the classical books offer least. The first book and the reference set out a complete grammar of combination, clash, punishment and harm — for the Stems and Branches of one chart, and for the decades and years that visit it. No classical author wrote a chapter applying that grammar between the charts of a husband and a wife. What follows is therefore built from three materials: the one old rule that does compare a husband's pillar with a wife's; the doctrine of combination and clash inside a chart, which the classical authors worked out with great care; and the license of the last chapter, by which another person's pillars act on one's own as a year's do. The reader should hold this chapter a little more loosely than the others. The relations it describes are real relations. Their use between two people is the tradition's practice, not its scripture.

### The one old rule

There is one, and it is a surprise. The Song collection 五行精纪 preserves it, and 三命通会 copies it into its chapter on kin:

> 凡是子午卯酉日生，主娶子午卯酉命妻，若妻申子辰生、丑生、甲己生者，皆不永。
>
> Whoever is born on a day of the Rat, Horse, Rabbit or Rooster is disposed to take a wife whose fate is of the Rat, Horse, Rabbit or Rooster. If the wife was born in a year of Monkey, Rat or Dragon, or of the Ox, or of 甲 or 己, none of these last.

The second sentence makes sense for one day only, and the Song text supplies it — suppose a man born on 甲子 — together with the principle: 或日支三六合，或干合，俱不得偕老, where there is three-harmony or six-harmony with the Day Branch, or combination with the Day Stem, they do not grow old together. Monkey, Rat and Dragon are the frame of his Day Branch. The Ox is its harmony partner. 己 combines with his Day Stem. The rule continues, in both versions, with the escape clause quoted in Chapter Six — if the overcoming has already been gone through, or the wife has been married before, it does not apply — and the Song text adds that some men lose wife after wife until they marry one whose year has nothing in common with their day, and only then are left in peace. The same collection states the rule from the woman's side in the same form. A woman born on a cardinal day is disposed to a husband of a cardinal year, and the other classes of Branch likewise, as a damaged phrase is best read; 若嫁日干合或支神三合六合者，俱不偕老 — and if she marries one who combines with her Day Stem, or whose Branch stands in three-harmony or six-harmony with hers, they do not grow old together.

Three things about this rule should be noticed before it is set aside.

It belongs to the age of transition. It reads the husband by his day, in the new manner, and the wife by her year, in the old; "her fate," 命, means the year she was born in. It is the matchmakers' comparison with one foot in each art.

It distrusts joining. Every modern handbook treats a harmony between a man's Day Branch and something in his wife's chart as the best news a comparison can bring. The only classical statute on the point says such marriages do not last. No reason is given, and the wording in the older text is tangled enough that it can be read as condemning the wives of the four cardinal years as well.

And nobody kept it. Zhang Nan does not mention it. Chen Su'an does not mention it. The Qianlong compilers, who hunted down every rule that judged a marriage from one character of his and one of hers, did not find it worth refuting. It survives as a fossil inside a compendium that collected everything.

This book does not use it as a rule. It keeps it as a warning, because the warning turns out to have a respectable descendant. That a combination is not automatically a good thing — that what is joined is also held — is one of the considered teachings of 滴天髓, and it will be needed before the chapter is over.

### Where two charts touch

Inside one chart, the first book weighted every relation by distance: adjacent pillars at full strength, pillars one apart weakly, Year and Hour hardly at all. Between two charts there is no adjacency. Nothing in his chart is next to anything in hers. What takes the place of distance is rank: which of his pillars is meeting which of hers, and what part of each life those pillars govern.

The four pairings of like with like are the natural beginning, because each of them is a domain the two people share.

| His pillar | Her pillar | What meets |
|---|---|---|
| Year | Year | The two lineages and generations: the families, the backgrounds, the public face of the match. This is the only pairing the marketplace ever looked at. |
| Month | Month | The two seasons and the two working lives: the climate each of them lives in, and the parents' houses they come from. |
| Day | Day | The two selves and the two rooms: Day Master with Day Master, spouse palace with spouse palace. The couple proper. |
| Hour | Hour | The two private worlds: children, the late years, whatever the two make together and keep out of public view. |

The crossed pairings say how one person's domain falls on another's. His Day against her Month is his self and his home ground meeting her work and her season; her Day against his Month is the reverse. A Day against a Year is one person's intimate ground meeting the other's family. A Day against an Hour is one person's self meeting the other's children and inner life.

For weight, this book's working rule — and it is a working rule, not a classical one — follows the first book's hierarchy of the pillars. The Day Pillars come first, since they are the persons. The Month Branches come next, since each is the commander of its chart and whatever it touches it touches with the season's force. The Years and Hours come last for the couple, though the Years may be first for the families. And one further rule, which is a caution more than a weighting. By the license of the last chapter a partner's Branch acts on a chart as a year's Branch would, and so the first book's rules for arriving Branches apply to it. A harmony or a combination transforms only on the conditions the reference sets out, in full for the Stems and more briefly for the Branches: the season of the chart that receives it must support the new element, the rest of that chart must not oppose it, and — a condition the reference states for the Stems, which this book extends to the Branches — nothing may clash with either party. Otherwise it bonds without transforming, and the two, in the reference's words, still pull toward each other, magnetically bonded, so that whatever happens to one affects the other. A frame whose missing Branch a partner supplies is completed as a year would complete it, with the difference that the year goes and the person stays. No classical author says either of these things of two charts. Both are what the license implies, and they should be held as that.

### Stem against Stem

The first contact is between the two Day Masters, and Chapter Eight has already read it as an exchange: which of the Ten Gods each is to the other. Two of those relations are also named relations between Stems, and the lore has much to say about them.

Where the two Day Masters are a combining pair, they are one of the five marriages of Chapter One in person: a yang Stem and the yin Stem it controls. 三命通会 gave each of the five a name and a character, and though it was describing a single chart that carries the pair, readers have always applied the portraits to couples. 甲 and 己 are 中正之合, the combination of the centered and upright: Yang Wood, humane and first among the Stems, with Yin Earth, settled, honest, and possessed of the power to bring things to life. 乙 and 庚 are 仁义之合, the combination of humaneness and duty: Yin Wood too yielding by itself, Yang Metal that does not bend, and between them the hard and the soft each supplying the other. Of these two the compendium says, in an aside, that a woman need have no fear of them. 丙 and 辛 are 威制之合, the combination of awe and command: the sun in its blaze and a Metal that the compendium describes as sharp and fond of severity. 丁 and 壬 are 淫慝之合, which can only be translated as it stands, the combination of concealed wantonness: a Water that no light reaches and a Fire that hides its own flame, and the person who carries them bright-eyed, quick to feeling and easily moved. 戊 and 癸 are 无情之合, the combination without feeling — Yang Earth an old and ugly husband, Yin Water a graceful young wife, joined and yet indifferent, as an old yang and a young yin must be.

These are character sketches and no more, and the last two show their age. What the reader can take from them is the common fact: where two Day Masters combine, those who compare charts have always read an attraction that needs no cause outside itself, and it runs across the controlling cycle, so that one of the two is the other's Direct Wealth and the other is the first one's Direct Officer. For a couple in which the yang Stem is the man's, the combination and the spouse stars say one thing. Where the yang Stem is the woman's, the combination is the same and the stars are reversed, and the last chapter described that couple.

Where the two Day Masters clash — 甲 with 庚, 乙 with 辛, 丙 with 壬, 丁 with 癸, the four pairs the tradition lists, three of which the first two texts had occasion to name — they are of one polarity and stand across the controlling cycle: each is the other's Seven Killings or Windfall. 滴天髓 calls a clash between Stems 天战, war in heaven, and ranks it below the war on earth: 天战犹自可，地战急如火 — heaven's war can still be borne; earth's war is urgent as fire. The old note explains that the Stems' part is to move and the Branches' part is to be still, so that a quarrel among Stems does little harm where the ground beneath them is quiet. Between two people the teaching is easily read. A clash between Day Masters is a difference of declared will: two people who say opposite things, want the lead in the same moment, and find each other bracing or exhausting according to what lies underneath. It is the palaces that decide whether such a pair can live together.

After the Day Masters come the other Stems. A combination between one person's Day Master and a Stem in another pillar of the partner's chart is a bond with the domain that pillar governs: with the family and the wider world at the Year, with the working life at the Month, with the children and the private world at the Hour. A clash there is a strain with the same domain. And a Stem that appears in both charts — the same character on his Month and on hers, his Day Master standing on her Hour — is a thing held in common, which each of them reads as a different god.

One configuration the texts single out. Where two Stems in one chart both combine with a single Stem in the other, the combination is contested: 争合. The format of the principal and the secondary wife in Chapter Seven was the same situation inside one chart, and the reading does not change when it lies between two. A Day Master wanted by two is pulled two ways; a Day Master that is one of the two is in a competition, with something or someone, for a partner who cannot be had whole.

### Branch against Branch

The second contact is between the two spouse palaces, and this is the one that matters most for daily life, because the palace is where each person lives. Chapter Three read the relations of a palace with its neighbours inside the chart. The same four relations, and the same readings, hold between his room and hers.

Where the two Day Branches stand in six-harmony, the rooms join. Each of the six pairs has its own temper, which the reference gives and Part Two repeats for couples — the loyal harmony of Rat and Ox, the warm one of Horse and Goat, and the rest — but what they share is that the two foundations pull toward each other and steady each other. A palace that was struck in its own chart is held by the partner's; a palace that stood alone is reached.

Where they belong to one three-harmony frame, the rooms share a direction. Two Branches of a frame, with the Peak among them, are the reference's coalition waiting for its third member; the couple lives toward a common element and completes it in the years that bring the missing Branch.

Where they are the same Branch, the rooms are one room. The palace is doubled: whatever it was for one of them it is now for both, with nothing in the other to vary it. Two people on the same ground understand each other's habits without explanation and share each other's blind side without relief. If the Branch is one of the four that punish themselves — Dragon, Horse, Rooster, Pig — the doubling is the self-punishment itself.

Where they clash, the rooms oppose. Everything Chapter Three said of a clashed palace now describes the home: it cannot keep still. But the two classical qualifications apply with full force. The first is that a clash has a winner, and between two charts each Branch brings its own season. 滴天髓 has the rule in two lines: 旺者冲衰衰者拔，衰者冲旺旺神发 — when the strong clashes the weak, the weak is uprooted; when the weak clashes the strong, the strong is roused. A person whose Day Branch is in command of its own chart, set against one whose Day Branch is abandoned by its season, is not in an equal contest. The second is the vaults. 三命通会's chapter on the clashes is plain that the relation is not to be judged in the lump:

> 相冲者，十二支战击之神，大概为凶。然有为福之甚者，乃冲处相生。
>
> The clashes are the twelve Branches as gods of battle, and in the main they are ill. Yet there are cases in which they are a very great blessing: where at the point of clash there is generation.

The compendium's instance is of two clashing pillars whose sound-elements generate one another, a reckoning this canon does not use; what is taken from it here is only that a clash is not judged in the lump. The same chapter adds that where the four storage Branches hold what a chart needs, 尤喜冲激 — a clash to rouse them is especially welcome. Dragon against Dog, Ox against Goat: on that view two people who both sit on vaults, and whose vaults stand opposite, bring out what was sealed in each other. Chapter Three named the doubters. Such couples are not quiet, on any view.

Where they stand in punishment or in harm, the rooms wear on each other, in the ways Chapter Three described: a pattern that repeats, or an alliance of the one quietly spoiled by the other.

Two combinations of Stem and Branch together have names. Where the Stems of the two Day Pillars combine and the Branches harmonize as well — 甲子 with 己丑, 壬子 with 丁丑 — the Song books call it 天地德合 (tiān dì dé hé), heaven and earth joined in virtue: the selves reach for each other and so do the rooms. They speak of it between two pillars of a single chart, and prize it most where one of them is the Hour; its use between the Day Pillars of two people is the handbooks'. For the few such pairs that fall within a single decade of the sixty-fold cycle they keep a grander name, 君臣庆会, ruler and minister in glad assembly; this book uses the first name for all of them. Every one of the sixty Day Pillars has exactly one such partner, and Part Two lists them. And where the Stems clash while the Branches clash — 甲寅 against 庚申, 壬子 against 丙午 — the old note to 滴天髓 calls it 天地交战, heaven and earth at war, and says of it inside one chart what need not be softened between two: ill, beyond doubt. Ren Tieqiao, as the next section but one will show, denies it even the relief that a combination brings to other clashes. It is the first book's turning moan, standing permanently between two people.

Beyond the palaces, every Branch of the one chart meets every Branch of the other, and each meeting is read by the rank of the pillars it joins. One class of these deserves its own heading.

### Frames that take two

A three-harmony frame needs three Branches, and a single chart holds four. Many charts therefore carry two members of a frame and lack the third, and the reference describes each such fragment: the half-frame with its Peak, a permanent readiness; the pair without its Peak, which is no frame at all but two ends cradling an absence. When the partner's chart holds the missing Branch, the frame is completed between them, as a year would complete it, for as long as the two are together.

Whether that is fortunate is decided exactly as Chapter Eight decided everything: by what the frame's element is to each of them. A Water frame completed between a man who needs Water and a woman who needs Water is the two of them, together, making what each lacks. A Fire frame completed between two people already scorched is the same mechanism feeding a common excess. The frame itself is neither. It is a power the couple has jointly and neither has alone, and the position of its three Branches says where: a frame whose Peak is one partner's Day Branch gathers on that person's seat, and that person is its center.

### What a combination can do, and what it cannot

The classical doctrine of combination stands on two pages of 滴天髓 and its old notes, with a third note on the vaults and one chapter of the old manual on a particular seat. The reading of this book's pair turns on them. They are given here whole, because each has a half that is usually left out.

It can still a clash. The first page is the one on war. Having said that a clash of Stems can be borne and a clash of Branches is urgent, the old note goes on:

> 其有两冲者，只得一个合神有力，或会神，或库神，或贵神，以收其动气，息其争气，亦有佳者。
>
> Where there are two clashes, it needs only one combining god with strength, or a gathering god, or a vault god, or a noble god, to gather in the moving breath and put the contending breath to rest, and such charts too can be good.

Four things, then, may quiet a struck Branch: the Branch in six-harmony with it, a Branch of its three-harmony frame, the vault of its element, and a Noble. The first of them must have strength. Of the war of heaven and earth together, Stems clashing above clashing Branches, the same note says 必凶无疑 and then allows one relief: 遇运岁合之会之，视其胜负 — where a decade or a year combines with one side or gathers with it, look to which side prevails. Ren Tieqiao is stricter. 天地交战，虽有合神会神，亦不息其动气: where heaven and earth are at war together, then though there be a combining god or a gathering one, the motion is not put to rest.

It can help, and it can tether. The second page is on combination itself. 合有宜不宜，合多不为奇: combination is sometimes fitting and sometimes not, and much combination is nothing to marvel at. The old note sorts the cases, four of each kind.

> 喜神有能合助之者，以庚为喜神，得乙合而助之，以其化金也；凶神有能合而去之者，以甲为凶神，得己合而去之，以其化土也；动局有能合而静者，如子午相冲，得丑合而静；生局有能合而成者，如甲生于亥，得寅合而成；皆宜也。
>
> A favorable god can be helped by a combination: where 庚 is the favorable god and 乙 combines with it, it is helped, because the pair turns to Metal. A harmful god can be removed by one: where 甲 is the harmful god and 己 combines with it, it is removed, because the pair turns to Earth. A configuration in motion can be stilled by one: as when Rat and Horse clash, and get an Ox to combine, and are still. A configuration of growth can be completed by one: as when 甲 is born in the Pig month and gets a Tiger to combine. All of these are fitting.

> 若助其凶神之合，如己为凶神，甲合之，则助己，以其化土也；绊其喜神之合，如乙为喜神，庚合之，则绊乙，以其化金也；或丑未为喜神，子午合之，则成闲神；或甲木为忌神，寅亥合之，则增凶势；皆不宜也。
>
> But the combination that helps a harmful god, as where 己 is harmful and 甲 combines with it, and helps it, because the pair turns to Earth; the combination that tethers a favorable god, as where 乙 is favorable and 庚 combines with it, and tethers it, because the pair turns to Metal; or the Ox or the Goat a favorable god, and the Rat or the Horse combining with it, so that it becomes an idle god; or 甲 the god to be shunned, and Tiger and Pig combining to swell its force: all of these are unfitting.

The test is the same in nearly every line, and the note repeats it like a refrain: because of what the pair turns toward. A combination is a kindness to a god when the pair leans toward that god's own element, and a tether when it leans elsewhere. The line about the Ox and the Goat is the exception that shows the other half of the rule: Rat and Ox lean toward Earth, which is the Ox's own element, and the Ox is an idle god all the same, because whatever else is true of it, a Branch that is combined is held. The note then closes on the general warning, 大率多合则不流通，不奋发，虽有秀气，亦不为奇: in the main, where there is much combining there is no flow and no rousing of the spirit, and though there be fine breath in the chart, it comes to nothing remarkable.

Ren Tieqiao tightens all of this. 若忌合而合之，比冲愈凶: a combination where combination was to be avoided is worse than a clash. A favorable god that combines and does not turn toward anything it can use is, in his words, 反为羁绊贪恋而无用矣 — only tethered, enamored, and of no use. And he reads the stilled clash strictly. It is fitting, he says, where 子午逢冲，喜神在午，得丑合之: where Rat and Horse clash, the favorable god is in the Horse, and an Ox combines. The Ox, that is, takes hold of the Rat that was doing the harm. His companion instance runs the other way: Tiger and Monkey clash, the favorable god is in the Tiger, and a Pig combines with it, turning with the Tiger toward the Tiger's own Wood. He gives a third instance to mark the limit of the first: 卯酉逢冲，喜神在卯，得辰合之，化金作克火者，皆是不宜也 — Rabbit and Rooster clash, the favorable god is in the Rabbit, and a Dragon combines with the Rooster; that too is unfitting, because Dragon and Rooster turn to Metal, which is what the Rooster was striking with, and the Metal goes on to strike the Fire. Taking hold of the attacker is not enough if the pair leans toward the attacker's weapon. The three instances together make a rule that Ren does not state in so many words, and this book states it as its own. A clash on a favorable Branch is rightly stilled in one of two ways: by a combination that takes hold of the Branch that strikes it and turns it away from the element it strikes with, or by one that joins the struck Branch to partners turning toward its own element. A combination that ties the favorable Branch to a partner turning elsewhere is the tether of the second list, and a combination that feeds the attacker's weapon is worse than none.

About the four vaults there is a third note, and it has to be read with the first two texts of this canon beside it. The line of 滴天髓 is 生方怕动库宜开, the places of birth dread being moved and the vaults ought to be opened, and the old note explains:

> 寅申巳亥，四生也；忌冲动。辰戌丑未，四库也；宜冲开。子午卯酉，四败也，有逢合而喜冲者，不若生地之必不可冲也；有逢冲而喜合者，不若库地之必不可闭也。
>
> Tiger, Monkey, Snake and Pig are the four places of birth, and they dread being clashed into motion. Dragon, Dog, Ox and Goat are the four vaults, and they ought to be clashed open. Rat, Horse, Rabbit and Rooster are the four places of defeat, the cardinal Branches. Some of them, meeting a combination, welcome a clash; they are not like the places of birth, which must never be clashed. Some of them, meeting a clash, welcome a combination; they are not like the vaults, which must never be shut.

Two things are in this note. A cardinal Branch that is clashed may welcome a combination, which is the general license for everything that will be said about his Rat. And a vault is a thing that can be shut, and should not be; what shuts it, the comparison implies, is the combination that a cardinal Branch may welcome. The old note on the unfitting cases says so in a word, in the text of it that Ren Tieqiao's edition prints: where the Ox or the Goat is the favorable god, and Rat or Horse combines with it, 则闭, it is shut. So the classical doctrine of the vaults is this: a clash opens them, a combination shuts them, and they are not to be shut. Shen Xiaozhan and Ren doubt the first half, as Chapter Three said, and hold that a clash between two vaults stirs the Earth they are made of and does not release what they store. No classical author doubts the second half.

The first book and the reference speak otherwise. Both count a combination, together with a clash, among the things that open a vault; the reference's entry on the Ox says that it releases what it holds only under a clash, a combination, or the slow turning of time. On this one point of mechanism the two earlier texts of this canon and the classical notes do not agree, and this canon follows the classics. This book therefore reads a combination with a vault as holding it, and the two earlier texts are to be brought into line with it, which is outside this book's pages. Nothing in the reading below turns on the point.

It can fill the Void. 三命通会's ruling was given in Chapter Three, and it has two halves that must be kept together. Where the blessings of a chart gather in a Branch that falls in the Void, the Void scatters them, and then 有空亡却喜见合，合则不能空矣 — a combination is to be welcomed, for what is combined can no longer be empty. Where what gathers there is harmful, the Void is the thing that dissolves it, and then 有空亡不宜见合: it ought not to be combined, for exactly the same reason. The passage also names the limiting case, 若无冲无合无刑，谓真空亡. Where there is no clash, no combination and no punishment, that is called the true Void: a Branch that is empty and that nothing reaches.

It can tie a Day Master to the wrong thing. A little further on from the page on combination the same book gives that condition a name. 绊神 (bàn shén): the tethering god. The verse is one of the few in that austere text that smiles:

> 出门要向天涯游，何似裙钗恣意留。
>
> Going out at the gate, he meant to roam to the edge of the sky — and what is this, lingering among skirts and hairpins at their pleasure?

and the note beneath says what it means in a chart: 日主有合，不顾用神 — the Day Master has its combination and takes no thought for its Useful God. He is attached, and the attachment is real, and it is to something other than what he needs. 皆有情而反无情, the note concludes: all feeling, and for that very reason, no feeling at all.

And one seat has a chapter of its own. The old manual's section on the Day Blade names its three pillars — 丙午, 戊午, 壬子 — and says of them 不喜刑冲破害，不喜会合: such a seat welcomes neither punishment, clash and harm, nor meeting and combination. 若四柱中一来会合，必主奇祸: let a combination once arrive among the four pillars, and strange calamity is sure. The compendium repeats the rule and spells out the combinations it means, 三会六合, the three-harmony and the six. The old manual's worked example is a general's chart in which a decade raised the Blade by a frame and a year then struck it, and its moral is that what a Blade cannot abide is 一合一冲, one combination and one clash together.

Against these stand two sentences of the same books. One is the maxim 阳刃怕冲，宜合迎, a Blade dreads the clash and is glad to meet a combination; Zhang Nan has it as 帮身阳刃，喜合嫌冲, said of the Blade that helps the self, and glosses the combination he means as the Blade's with the Seven Killings. The other is the compendium's own summing-up, after it has set the lines that dread a Blade beside the line that calls a Blade no evil to a Day Stem without vigor. 义各有取, it says: each has its application. The first speak of a strong self that can carry its Wealth and wants no robber. The second speaks of a weak self that cannot, and is glad of a brother. On this book's reading the Day-Blade chapter is written of the first kind, since what it asks for the seat is the Seven Killings to govern it and the Officer's country to walk in, which are the wants of a strong self. The reading is this book's. The compendium's one qualification of the rule against clashing or combining a Blade runs the other way from the weak chart's hope: 命元浅薄，遇此诚然，若命旺秉气深厚，或有天月德及赦文解救，止有浮实，亦无大咎 — a chart of shallow resources finds the warning true, and one of deep vigor, or with the virtue stars or a pardon to relieve it, meets only surface trouble and no great harm. And it adds, of a Blade that is already clashed or combined in the natal chart, that a decade and a year arriving to clash or combine it again are 大凶, gravely ill, while one of the two alone is half that. A weak chart seated on a clashed Blade should read those sentences before it welcomes a combination, and this book has read them.

So a comparison of two charts by contact alone can be exactly wrong. It counts the combinations and calls the total a score. The classical doctrine asks of each combination what it takes hold of, what the pair leans toward, and whether the thing held was wanted free. A harmony that takes hold of a hostile Branch is a blessing. A combination that ties a Day Master to the element it was already drowning in is a tether, and the more delightful for being one.

### The charts

Here are all the contacts between the two charts of this book, pillar by pillar.

| | Her Year 庚申 | Her Month 己卯 | Her Day 丙申 | Her Hour 庚寅 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| His Year 丙辰 | Branches cradle the Water frame | Branches in harm; Branches in one direction | the same Stem; Branches cradle the Water frame | Branches in one direction |
| His Month 甲午 | Stems clash | Stems combine | — | Stems clash; Branches a half of the Fire frame |
| His Day 壬子 | Branches a half of the Water frame | Branches in punishment | Stems clash; Branches a half of the Water frame | — |
| His Hour 癸卯 | — | the same Branch | — | Branches in one direction |

Read the diagonal first, like with like. At the Years, Dragon and Monkey: the two ends of the Water frame, the spring and the vault, cradling the river that neither Year contains. At the Months, two Stems that combine, his Yang Wood with her Yin Earth, over two Branches that have nothing to say to each other. At the Days, two Stems that clash over two Branches that gather toward one element: his Rat is the Peak of her Monkey's frame. At the Hours, Rabbit and Tiger, neighbours in the calendar, two of the three Branches of the East. The pattern is a strange one, and the reader should see it whole before any part of it is weighed. Heaven, in this pair, is noisy: a clash at the center, a clash and a combination at the Months, and the same character at the head of his chart and at the head of hers. Earth is settled where it counts and uneasy at the edges.

Now the center, since this is what the book has been approaching for eight chapters.

The two Day Masters clash. 丙 against 壬, Yang Fire against Yang Water: war in heaven, in the verse's word, which 天战犹自可, can still be borne. Chapter Eight said what the two are to each other — each the other's spouse star in person, in the charge the fable does not marry — and the compendium said what yang meeting yang is: two that contend. The old note's reason for bearing it is that the Stems' part is to move and the Branches' part to be still, so that a quarrel among Stems does little harm where the ground is quiet. So the question the clash leaves is the one this chapter put at its head. It is the palaces that decide whether such a pair can live together.

His palace is the Rat, clashed at full strength by the Horse of his own month: the first book's defining interaction, and what Chapter Five called a room that cannot be still. Her palace is the Monkey, clashed at full strength by the Tiger of her own hour, the room that cannot be still either. Monkey and Rat are two Branches of one frame, the Water frame, and the Rat is its Peak: by the reference's rule they are a half-frame, a coalition waiting for its third member. The third member is the Dragon, and the Dragon is his Year Branch.
